## Abstract {#abstract}

Fraud detection in FinTech platforms faces significant challenges including extreme class imbalance, evolving bot-driven attack strategies, and the need for operationally viable precision-constrained decision thresholds. This dissertation presents an end-to-end machine learning framework that combines behavioral features (keystroke dynamics, session timing, network indicators) with transactional features (amounts, balances, transaction types) calibrated from the PaySim dataset to detect fraudulent transactions. A hybrid synthetic data generation pipeline with enhanced multimodal calibration produces realistic datasets across four user profiles, including standard and stealth bots, enabling controlled experimentation without access to proprietary financial data. The approach demonstrates robust performance stability across distributional variations, validating its practical reliability for real-world deployment.

Four classification algorithms (DummyClassifier, Logistic Regression, Random Forest, Gradient Boosting) are systematically evaluated across three feature sets (behavioral-only, transactional-only, combined) using strict train/validation/test separation, with thresholds tuned exclusively on the validation set under a precision constraint. An initial precision ≥ 0.90 constraint reveals the severity of strict precision requirements; a systematic sweep identifies precision ≥ 0.50 as the optimal operating point, under which all models are re-trained across five random seeds for statistical robustness.

Results demonstrate that behavioral features achieve strong bot detection (ROC-AUC 0.919 ± 0.015, PR-AUC 0.852 ± 0.026 for Random Forest), while transactional features alone provide moderate fraud discrimination (ROC-AUC 0.711–0.717). Combining both feature types improves fraud detection PR-AUC by \~38% for ensemble methods, confirming the complementary value of multi-modal feature fusion. Under the optimized precision ≥ 0.50 constraint, Combined Gradient Boosting achieves recall of 52.4% ± 6.5% and F1 of 0.524 ± 0.020 across five seeds. Random Forest and Gradient Boosting perform comparably, both substantially outperforming Logistic Regression for the fraud detection task. The framework provides actionable guidance for threshold selection under different operational risk tolerances.

## **Keywords:** ML, fraud, fintech, behavioral, transactions, keystroke dynamics, session timing, network indicators, amounts, balances, transaction types, Random Forest, Gradient Boosting

## 1\. Introduction {#1.-introduction}

### 1.1 Context and Motivation {#1.1-context-and-motivation}

The rapid expansion of digital payments and FinTech platforms has fundamentally reshaped the financial services landscape. Mobile money transfers, online lending, digital wallets, and instant payment rails now process billions of transactions annually, enabling unprecedented convenience for consumers and businesses alike. However, this digital transformation has simultaneously created new attack surfaces for financial fraud. Global fraud losses continue to escalate, with the European Central Bank (2022) reporting significant increases in unauthorized payment transactions, while Europol's Internet Organised Crime Threat Assessment (Europol, 2023\) identifies online fraud as one of the fastest-growing criminal activities in the European Union.

A particularly concerning trend is the growing role of automated bots in perpetrating fraud at scale. Bot-driven attacks, including account takeover (ATO), credential stuffing, fake account creation, and automated transaction fraud, exploit the speed and anonymity of digital channels to execute thousands of fraudulent operations per minute (Imperva, 2023). The Imperva Bad Bot Report (2023) estimates that approximately 30% of all internet traffic is generated by malicious bots, with sophisticated "stealth bots" increasingly capable of mimicking human behavior to evade detection. In the FinTech context, these bots can automate the entire fraud lifecycle from account compromise to fund extraction, making traditional rule-based detection systems increasingly incapable to adapt.

The limitations of static, rule-based fraud detection, with rigid thresholds, inability to adapt to evolving attack patterns, and high false-positive rates, have motivated the adoption of machine learning approaches that can learn complex, non-linear patterns from data (Carcillo et al., 2018; Hilal et al., 2022). However, fraud detection poses significant methodological challenges: extreme class imbalance (fraud rates often below 1%), the need for precision-constrained thresholds to minimize customer friction, and the requirement for models that can integrate multiple signal types. These challenges motivate the research presented in this dissertation.

### 1.2 Problem Statement {#1.2-problem-statement}

This dissertation addresses the problem of detecting fraudulent transactions in FinTech platforms by combining behavioral and transactional signals within a supervised machine learning framework. While transactional features (amounts, balances, transaction types) have been the traditional basis for fraud detection, they provide limited discrimination when fraud rates are extremely low and attackers deliberately structure transactions to resemble legitimate activity. Behavioral features, derived from how users interact with the platform (keystroke timing, mouse movements, session dynamics, network indicators), offer a complementary signal that is harder for attackers to forge. The central question is whether fusing these two signal types into a single model yields measurably better fraud detection than using either alone.

Two additional challenges compound the detection problem. First, extreme class imbalance causes standard classifiers to trivially predict the majority class, requiring specialized metrics (PR-AUC over accuracy), sampling strategies, and class-weighted objectives. Second, operational threshold selection must balance precision against recall. In practice, fraud detection systems must balance a high precision constraint without heavily affecting the recall, i.e, being capable to detect the most fraudulent transactions while minimizing customer friction or false positive flagging. Within the scope of this project is to determine an optimal suggested threshold range for sustainable operation.

### 1.3 Research Questions {#1.3-research-questions}

#### RQ1 — Behavioral detection effectiveness {#rq1-—-behavioral-detection-effectiveness}

- How effectively can behavioral features (session dynamics, keystroke timing, mouse patterns, network indicators) distinguish bot-driven from human-driven transactions in a FinTech platform?

#### RQ2 — Transactional detection effectiveness {#rq2-—-transactional-detection-effectiveness}

- How effectively can transactional features (amounts, balance changes, transaction types) identify fraudulent transactions when calibrated against real-world distributions (PaySim)?

#### RQ3 — Combined model value {#rq3-—-combined-model-value}

- Does combining behavioral and transactional features into a single model improve fraud detection quality (precision, recall, PR-AUC) compared to using either feature set alone?

#### RQ4 — Model comparison and threshold sensitivity {#rq4-—-model-comparison-and-threshold-sensitivity}

- How do different classification algorithms (Random Forest, Logistic Regression, Gradient Boosting) compare in detection quality, and how sensitive are operational decisions to threshold selection under a precision constraint?

### 1.4 Hypotheses {#1.4-hypotheses}

- **H1 — Behavioral features detect bots**: Behavioral features alone will achieve strong bot detection performance, with stealth bots being harder to detect than standard bots.
- **H2 — Transactional features detect fraud**: Transactional features calibrated from PaySim distributions, combined with derived risk indicators (balance drain ratio, destination balance anomalies), will achieve meaningful fraud detection (ROC-AUC \> 0.70).
- **H3 — Combined superiority**: A combined model using both feature sets will outperform either transactional model in ROC-AUC and recall at the precision constraint.
- **H4 — Algorithm comparison**: Random Forest will outperform Gradient Boosting and Logistic Regression on ROC-AUC for the fraud detection task due to its ensemble diversity and robustness to overfitting on noisy labels.

### 1.5 Scope {#1.5-scope}

The scope of this research is to design, implement, and evaluate a predictive model for fraud detection that leverages both transactional and behavioral patterns in FinTech platforms.

**In scope:**

- Synthetic dataset generation calibrated from real-world data (PaySim)
- Behavioral feature engineering for bot detection
- Transactional feature engineering for fraud detection
- Multi-algorithm comparison (baseline, linear, ensemble methods)
- Threshold tuning under operational precision constraints
- Statistical validation of results across multiple seeds

**Expected outcome:** A scoring model that returns a fraud probability score per transaction and a recommended decision (ALLOW / REVIEW / DENY) based on a tuned threshold.

**Out of scope:**

- Backend architecture (REST vs event-driven)
- Real-time streaming or production deployment
- Real-world user testing
- Deep learning or advanced NLP-based approaches
- Integration with external fraud databases or device fingerprinting services

### 1.6 Contributions {#1.6-contributions}

This dissertation makes the following contributions:

1. **A hybrid synthetic data generation pipeline** that combines PaySim-calibrated transactional features with literature-grounded behavioral features across four user profiles (human_normal, human_fast, bot_standard, bot_stealth), enabling controlled experimentation on multi-modal fraud detection without access to proprietary data.
2. **Systematic evaluation of feature fusion for fraud detection**, comparing behavioral-only, transactional-only, and combined feature sets across four algorithms, demonstrating that adding behavioral features to transactional models improves ROC-AUC by approximately 14% and PR-AUC by approximately 47% for ensemble methods.
3. **A rigorous threshold tuning methodology** using strict train/validation/test separation, where thresholds are selected exclusively on the validation set under a precision constraint, preventing optimistic bias.
4. **Multi-seed statistical validation** across five independent dataset generations and model training runs, reporting mean ± standard deviation for all metrics to demonstrate reproducibility and robustness of findings.
5. **A precision-constraint sweep analysis** (0.40–0.80) that quantifies the precision-recall tradeoff for operational deployment, providing actionable guidance on threshold selection for different risk tolerance levels.

### 1.7 Repository {#1.7-repository}

The implementation is publicly available at: `[https://github.com/Gisellev14/Meng-Computer-Science-Thesis#]`

### 1.8 Dissertation Structure {#1.8-dissertation-structure}

The remainder of this dissertation is organized as follows:

- **Chapter 2 — Foundations** introduces the foundational concepts necessary to understand the research, including online fraud typologies and the regulatory landscape, bot-driven fraud taxonomy and behavioral biometrics, supervised classification for imbalanced data, and synthetic data generation approaches.
- **Chapter 3 — Related Work** surveys the existing literature on fraud detection in financial systems, behavioral biometrics and bot detection, combined behavioral-transactional approaches, ensemble methods for fraud detection, and identifies the research gaps addressed by this work.
- **Chapter 4 — Methodology and Implementation** describes the technical approach, including synthetic data generation calibrated from PaySim, behavioral and transactional feature engineering, the model training pipeline with threshold tuning, and the statistical robustness protocol.
- **Chapter 5 — Evaluation and Results** presents the experimental findings, including distribution validation of the synthetic data, model performance comparisons across all configurations, threshold sensitivity analysis, stealth bot detection analysis, feature importance rankings, ablation insights, and a comprehensive discussion addressing each research question and hypothesis.
- **Chapter 6 — Conclusion** summarizes the key findings and contributions, discusses limitations, and outlines directions for future work.

## 2\. Foundations / Background {#2.-foundations-/-background}

In this chapter we will cover the foundational concepts related to online fraud in FinTech and bot-driven fraud. The purpose of this is to provide a broad understanding of the topic and establish the theoretical basis for the methods used in this dissertation.

### 2.1 Online Fraud in FinTech {#2.1-online-fraud-in-fintech}

Fintech platforms have transformed payment systems and financial services through digital innovation, but this transformation has also created new attack surfaces for fraud. The convergence of high transaction volumes, remote onboarding, cross-border flows, and rapid product innovation enables sophisticated fraud schemes that exploit both technical vulnerabilities and regulatory gaps (Anderson & Moore, 2006; Levi & Burrows, 2007; European Central Bank, 2022).

#### 2.1.1 Fraud Typology {#2.1.1-fraud-typology}

Fintech fraud spans multiple attack vectors. Common fraud types include:

- **Payment fraud and Authorized Push Payment (APP) schemes:** Victims are socially engineered into authorizing fraudulent transfers.
- **Corporate accounting and reporting fraud:** Fabricated revenues and round-tripping transactions, as exemplified by the Wirecard collapse which revealed €1.9 billion in fabricated assets (McCrum, 2023).
- **Electronic money-laundering:** Exploiting high transaction volumes and opacity in customer relationships to layer illicit funds (Levi & Burrows, 2007).

The enabling features of fintech platforms, such as rapid product innovation, pseudonymous or remote onboarding, cross-border payment rails, and insufficient regulatory perimetering during early growth phases, create opportunities that traditional banking controls may not address.

#### 2.1.2 Fraud Lifecycle {#2.1.2-fraud-lifecycle}

A synthesized fraud lifecycle commonly includes five stages, which reinforces the need for detection mechanisms across multiple dimensions (Levi & Burrows, 2007):

1. **Setup and onboarding:** Attackers create accounts, shell entities, or fake partners to establish transaction rails.
2. **Execution and exploitation:** Fraudulent transactions or APP social-engineering are executed to move value.
3. **Concealment and layering:** Layering transactions, round-tripping, and complex corporate relationships obscure origins and beneficiaries.
4. **Monetization and exit:** Funds are extracted to beneficiaries or converted to other assets via rapid cross-border transfers.
5. **Detection and enforcement:** Regulatory reviews, audits, and law enforcement intervene; failures in oversight can delay detection significantly.

#### 2.1.3 Industry Impact and Regulatory Landscape {#2.1.3-industry-impact-and-regulatory-landscape}

Financial fraud has significant economic and societal impact, with global losses reaching billions annually. High-profile failures damage trust in fintech offerings and may slow adoption or prompt tighter market scrutiny.

The European regulatory framework imposes several requirements relevant to fraud detection:

- **PSD2 (Payment Services Directive 2):** Requires Strong Customer Authentication (SCA) using two-factor authentication from knowledge, possession, and inherence categories (European Commission, 2015; EBA, 2018). It also defines liability allocation between payment service providers for unauthorized transactions.
- **Anti-Money Laundering (AML) directives:** Impose customer due diligence, transaction monitoring, and reporting obligations for suspicious activity.
- **BaFin oversight:** The German federal financial supervisory authority provides national supervision through licensing, governance expectations, and enforcement actions. The Wirecard case highlighted supervisory shortcomings and prompted reform proposals.
- **GDPR:** While not fraud-specific, the General Data Protection Regulation constrains fraud detection practices through requirements for data minimization, purpose limitation, and individual rights.

### 2.2 Bot-Driven Fraud {#2.2-bot-driven-fraud}

Bot-driven fraud represents a particularly challenging threat vector in fintech. Automated attacks range from simple scripted bots executing credential stuffing at scale to advanced systems capable of mimicking human behavior (Egele et al., 2012). These attacks exploit the tension between security and user experience: overly aggressive fraud controls create friction that drives customer abandonment, while lenient controls allow fraud losses to escalate.

#### 2.2.1 Bot Taxonomy {#2.2.1-bot-taxonomy}

Bots can be classified by their sophistication level:

- **Scripted bots:** Scripted bots typically exhibit significantly lower variability in interaction timing compared to humans, who display natural variability. For example, coefficients of variation for typing rhythm are often reported to be below \~0.04, while humans show an observable higher variability (eg., CV \> \~0.30) due to natural neuromotor fluctuations (Ahmed & Traore, 2007). Additionally, scripted bots exhibit lower keystroke dwell times and unnaturally high request rates.
- **Sophisticated bots:** Headless browsers (e.g., Puppeteer, Playwright) that attempt to slow down execution and introduce randomness. While these bots may approximate average timing characteristics, they often retain lower variability and more regular patterns than human users (Egele et al., 2012).
- **AI-powered bots:** Machine learning-driven automation that learns to replicate human behavioral distributions. These bots successfully match many human metrics, such as mean flight time \~140ms vs human \~150ms, but still show slightly reduced variance or lack subtle irregularities found in genuine human interaction (Acien et al., 2022).

#### 2.2.2 Bot Objectives in FinTech {#2.2.2-bot-objectives-in-fintech}

Bot-driven fraud in fintech typically involves:

- **Account takeover (ATO):** Automated credential stuffing using leaked username/password databases.
- **Fake account creation:** Automated account openings using fabricated or stolen identities to establish transaction rails.
- **Card testing:** Rapid low-value transactions to validate stolen card numbers.
- **Transaction automation:** High-volume automated transfers for money laundering or fund extraction.

Bots often operate as coordinated clusters with multi-step or multi-account money movement, motivating graph-based detection to expose ties among accounts and devices (Akoglu et al., 2014).

#### 2.2.3 Behavioral Signals {#2.2.3-behavioral-signals}

Behavioral biometrics provide continuous, passive signals that are difficult to replicate. Key discriminative features include:

- **Keystroke dynamics:** Flight time, dwell time, and typing rhythm consistency. Prior work reports that extremely low flight times, often below \~70–80 ms, are associated with automated input, whereas human users typically exhibit higher averages and greater variability due to cognitive and motor constraints (Ahmed & Traore, 2007).
- **Mouse movement patterns:** Trajectory curvature, speed profiles, acceleration variance, and micro-hesitations. Humans produce smooth, curved movements with bell-shaped velocity profiles, while bots often exhibit linear or overly precise strokes.
- **Session-level patterns:** Session duration, checkout velocity, pages visited, and request rates. Bots typically operate at significantly higher request rates than human users. Industry reports have found that automated systems complete actions in seconds with sustained request rates around 2-5 req/s, whereas human interaction rates are significantly lower (\~0.45 req/s) due to cognitive limitations (Imperva, 2023; Cloudflare, 2023).
- **Network indicators:** Datacenter IP usage, impossible travel flags from rotating proxies, and device fingerprint anomalies. Industry reports estimate that a substantial proportion of malicious bots traffic originated from datacenter environments rather than residential networks (Imperva, 2023).

#### 2.2.4 Machine Learning Approaches for Bot Detection {#2.2.4-machine-learning-approaches-for-bot-detection}

Supervised, unsupervised and deep learning techniques are all applied in fintech fraud detection; each addresses different facets of bot-detection and together form layered defenses. Literature describes supervised classifiers for account-opening fraud, clustering/anomaly methods for outlier detection, and deep/graph models for complex pattern recognition.

- **Supervised learning Classifiers:** trained on labeled fraud vs legitimate examples are crucial for account-opening and transaction decisioning; objective and loss formulation to handle extreme class imbalance is a central methodological focus.
- **Unsupervised learning:** Clustering and anomaly-detection approaches are used to surface novel or rare automated behaviors without labels, supporting AML and early-warning detection.
- **Deep learning and graph models:** LSTM and graph-based deep-learning architectures capture temporal sequences and relational patterns useful against staged and coordinated bot schemes, improving detection of synthetic and multi-step frauds.
- **Threat-intelligence fusion:** Recent works suggest that combining model outputs with external feeds and API-level intelligence helps to adapt detectors and proactively mitigate emerging automated attack patterns.

### 2.3 Supervised Classification for Imbalanced Data {#2.3-supervised-classification-for-imbalanced-data}

Fraud detection is inherently a binary classification problem under extreme class imbalance, with fraud rates typically below 1–2% of transactions (He & Garcia, 2009; Aguiar et al., 2022). This imbalance creates challenges for standard machine learning algorithms that optimize overall accuracy, leading to models that achieve high accuracy by simply predicting all transactions as legitimate while missing most fraud .

#### 2.3.1 Evaluation Metrics {#2.3.1-evaluation-metrics}

Studies have demonstrated that standard accuracy is misleading under class imbalance. Therefore, strategy effectiveness depends heavily on the chosen metric. The following metrics are used in this work:

- **Precision:** The fraction of flagged transactions that are truly fraudulent. High precision minimizes false positives and unnecessary customer friction.
- **Recall (sensitivity):** The fraction of all fraudulent transactions that are correctly detected. High recall minimizes missed fraud.
- **F1-score:** The harmonic mean of precision and recall, balancing both concerns. Theoretical work ties cost-sensitive approaches to bounds on achievable F-measure.
- **PR-AUC (Precision-Recall Area Under the Curve):** Preferred under extreme class imbalance because it reflects performance on the minority class and correlates with practical detection utility. Unlike ROC-AUC, which can be misleadingly optimistic under severe imbalance, PR-AUC directly measures the precision-recall tradeoff (Davis & Goadrich, 2006).
- **ROC-AUC (Receiver Operating Characteristic AUC):** Measures discriminative ability across all thresholds. Useful for comparing models but can overstate performance when the negative class vastly outnumbers the positive class.

#### 2.3.2 Threshold Tuning {#2.3.2-threshold-tuning}

Most classifiers output a probability score rather than a binary decision. The decision threshold converts these probabilities into actionable predictions (fraud vs. legitimate). The default threshold of 0.5 is often suboptimal under class imbalance (Elkan, 2001). Threshold selection strategies include:

- **Precision-constrained optimization:** Maximizing recall subject to a minimum precision, reflecting real-world constraints.
- **Cost-sensitive thresholding:** Selecting the cutoff that minimizes expected operational cost given asymmetric false positive and false negative costs.
- **ROC/Youden-style selection:** Choosing the threshold that maximizes the Youden index (sensitivity \+ specificity − 1\) on validation data.

The threshold must be selected on a validation set separate from the test set to avoid optimistic bias.

#### 2.3.3 Ensemble Methods {#2.3.3-ensemble-methods}

Ensemble methods combine multiple base learners to improve predictive performance and robustness:

- **Bagging (Random Forest):** Trains multiple decision trees on bootstrap samples and aggregates predictions via majority voting. Random Forests provide built-in feature importance, robustness to overfitting, and strong performance on tabular data. They handle class imbalance through class-weighted loss functions (Breiman, 2001).
- **Boosting (Gradient Boosting):** Sequentially trains weak learners that correct the errors of previous models. Gradient Boosting machines (including XGBoost and LightGBM) often achieve top performance in fraud detection benchmarks by focusing learning on hard-to-classify examples (Friedman, 2001).

Ensemble strategies targeting imbalance (e.g., EasyEnsemble, hybrid sampling+ensemble pipelines) have demonstrated gains in both ROC-AUC and PR-AUC compared with single classifiers.

### 2.4 Synthetic Data for Fraud Research {#2.4-synthetic-data-for-fraud-research}

Synthetic data generation addresses fundamental challenges in fraud detection research: extreme data scarcity, privacy constraints, and class imbalance (Douzas & Bacao, 2018).

#### 2.4.1 Motivation {#2.4.1-motivation}

Real fraud datasets commonly suffer from several constraints that motivate synthetic generation:

- **Privacy and regulatory constraints:** Transactional and identity data are confidential; sharing is tightly controlled by regulators and institutions, limiting collaborative research.
- **Data scarcity of fraud modes:** Fraud is rare and emergent attack types are underrepresented; models trained on available records may not generalize to new fraud patterns.
- **Extreme class imbalance:** Fraud ratios are often a small minority compared to legitimate transactions, causing models to bias toward the majority class without augmentation or special loss functions.
- **Label noise and concept drift:** Fraud labeling can be delayed, noisy, or changed with attacker behavior, degrading training signals.

#### 2.4.2 PaySim Simulator {#2.4.2-paysim-simulator}

PaySim (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016\) is an agent-based simulator that generates synthetic mobile money transaction logs calibrated from real financial transaction data. It simulates five transaction types (CASH_IN, CASH_OUT, DEBIT, PAYMENT, TRANSFER) with realistic transaction volumes, amounts following log-normal distributions, and a fraud rate of approximately 0.13%. Fraud in PaySim occurs exclusively in TRANSFER and CASH_OUT transactions, with 97.6% of fraudulent transfers draining the origin account to zero.

PaySim has become a widely used benchmark dataset in fraud detection research due to its realistic distributions and known ground truth labels, making it suitable for calibrating synthetic data generation pipelines.

#### 2.4.3 Generation Approaches {#2.4.3-generation-approaches}

Multiple approaches exist for generating synthetic fraud data:

| Method                 | Mechanism                           | Strengths                                          | Weaknesses                           |
| :--------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| Agent-based simulation | Simulates actors and interactions   | Realistic temporal and relational patterns         | Complex to calibrate and validate    |
| Parametric sampling    | Draws from fitted distributions     | Simple, fast, controlled                           | May miss complex joint distributions |
| GANs / CTGAN           | Adversarial generator/discriminator | Complex distributions, realistic minority examples | Mode collapse, unstable training     |
| SMOTE variants         | Feature-space interpolation         | Simple, fast, improves classifiers                 | Linear points, may amplify noise     |
| Diffusion models       | Iterative denoising                 | Strong fidelity for mixed-type data                | Emerging, limited evidence           |

This work uses parametric sampling calibrated from PaySim parameters, combining agent-based profile definitions with distribution-fitted transaction features.

#### 2.4.4 Validation of Synthetic Data {#2.4.4-validation-of-synthetic-data}

The literature evaluates synthetic fraud data quality through:

- **Downstream utility metrics:** Improvements (or degradations) in classifier performance (AUC, recall, precision, F1) on real held-out test sets.
- **Statistical similarity:** Distribution checks (KS tests, QQ plots) ensuring generated marginal and conditional relationships resemble real data.
- **Diversity and mode coverage:** Checks for mode collapse and whether synthetic sets cover rare categorical combinations.
- **Train on Synthetic, Test on Real (TSTR):** Evaluating whether models trained on synthetic samples generalize to real fraud instances.

Always evaluating on untouched real data is essential when measuring the utility of synthetic augmentation.

---

## 3\. Related Work {#3.-related-work}

### 3.1 Fraud Detection in Financial Systems {#3.1-fraud-detection-in-financial-systems}

Fraud detection in financial systems has evolved from rule-based systems to increasingly sophisticated machine learning approaches.

Rule-based systems are interpretable but limited in adaptability. Machine learning models such as Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and Gradient Boosting have demonstrated superior performance on fraud datasets (He & Garcia, 2009; Breiman, 2001; Friedman, 2001).

#### Traditional Rule-Based Approaches {#traditional-rule-based-approaches}

Early fraud detection systems relied on expert-defined rules (e.g., velocity checks, blacklists, transaction amount thresholds) to flag suspicious activity. While simple to implement and interpret, rule-based systems suffer from high false positive rates, inability to adapt to novel attack patterns, and difficulty scaling to complex multi-dimensional feature spaces. They remain valuable as first-line filters in multi-stage detection architectures, catching obvious fraud with near-zero false positives at sub-millisecond latency.

#### Machine Learning Approaches {#machine-learning-approaches}

Ensemble models have generally demonstrated to provide better fraud detection than standalone classical models, however between them, the best choice depends on whether we want to prioritize recall or low false positives. Recent studies have identified the following strengths for some common models:

- **Logistic Regression:** Provides interpretable coefficients and calibrated probabilities, serving as a strong baseline. On the other hand, are usually outperformed by ensembles on minority-class recall/AUC.
- **Random Forests:** Ensemble of bagged decision trees providing feature importance rankings, robustness to overfitting, and strong performance on tabular fraud data. Consistently strong, and sometimes best overall or second only to boosting methods.
- **Gradient Boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM):** Sequential boosting that focuses on hard-to-classify examples, often achieving top benchmark performance. Reported to often achieve the top accuracy/F1 and robustness on highly imbalanced online payment data.
- **Deep Learning:** LSTM, GRU and other neural networks demonstrated a high potential for fraud detection, especially on achieving higher recall values, though at the cost of precision.

Unsupervised approaches (isolation forests, one-class SVMs, autoencoders) complement supervised methods by surfacing novel or rare automated behaviors without labels, supporting AML and early-warning detection.

#### Handling Class Imbalance {#handling-class-imbalance}

Fraud datasets often present severe class imbalance, where the number of legitimate transactions far exceeds fraudulent ones, causing standard learners to focus on the majority class and ignore the rare events, which are the interesting ones. The literature reports several strategies for addressing the extreme class imbalance, grouped by families of techniques (Khan et al., 2023; Elreedy et al., 2024):

##### Data-level methods {#data-level-methods}

- **Random oversampling and undersampling:** Balance the classes by either duplicating minority samples or removing majority samples. It is a simple method but can lead to data loss and overfitting.
- **SMOTE and variants (SMOTE-ENN, ADASYN):** synthetically generate minority samples and clean noisy ones. Literature shows that often improve recall/F1 for fraud class versus not balancing the dataset.
- **GAN-based oversampling:** Generate synthetic minority samples using generative adversarial networks. Can outperform SMOTE-style methods, though at a significantly higher computational cost.

##### Algorithm-level methods {#algorithm-level-methods}

- **Cost-sensitive learning:** penalizes minority-class errors more heavily. Adaptive cost-sensitive approaches consistently improve minority-class performance without changing the data distribution.

##### Hybrid and ensemble methods {#hybrid-and-ensemble-methods}

- **Ensemble methods \+ resampling:** Hybrid approaches combining ensembles such as Random Forest or boosting with resampling techniques are consistently strong across different domains.
- **SMOTE / ROS \+ ensembles:** Some studies have found that traditional oversampling combined with ensembles can rival or beat GAN-based augmentation while having much lower processing costs.

#### Threshold Optimization {#threshold-optimization}

Choosing the right decision threshold can dramatically change precision, recall, and fairness without retraining or resampling. Techniques span simple metric-based tuning to more advanced, model‑agnostic frameworks. Many studies report the _best_ threshold as the one that maximizes the selected metric (e.g., F1-score, Youden's J statistic) on validation data, often by grid or explicit search over all candidate thresholds. For production environments, the literature finds that the default threshold of 0.5 is often suboptimal in imbalanced settings, though recent studies have shown that thresholds optimized for F1 or G-Mean consistently have beaten this default as well as outperform using the positive class prior alone, even when the prior is a strong baseline (Hancock et al., 2022; Leevy et al., 2018).

**Common strategies (binary, imbalanced):**

| Strategy                                         | Idea & typical use                                                            |
| :----------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Maximize F1 / G‑mean / MCC                       | Balance precision–recall or TPR–TNR; popular in fraud/credit scoring          |
| ROC‑based "closest to (0,1)"                     | Choose point with high sensitivity & specificity                              |
| Cost‑sensitive thresholds (THORS, GA, etc.)      | Optimize expected misclassification cost given cost matrix                    |
| Constrained search (e.g., TPR ≥ TNR, target FAR) | Enforce domain constraints like minimum sensitivity or fixed false alarm rate |

###

### 3.2 Behavioral Biometrics and Bot Detection {#3.2-behavioral-biometrics-and-bot-detection}

Behavioral biometrics are being increasingly integrated with machine learning for real-time fraud detection and to differentiate automated from human interactions.

#### Keystroke Dynamics {#keystroke-dynamics}

Keystroke timing patterns provide strong discriminative signals between humans and bots. Large-scale studies using 136 million keystroke events from 168,000 subjects demonstrate that detectors trained on massive labeled sets can reliably detect synthetic keystroke data, while few-shot scenarios remain challenging. Systems combining keystroke and mouse biometrics in authentication tasks have achieved top accuracies of approximately 96.95% for credential-stuffing detection.

Literature-reported benchmarks for keystroke features:

| Feature          | Human (mean ± σ) | Scripted Bot (mean ± σ) | Stealth Bot (mean ± σ) | AI Bot (mean ± σ) |
| :--------------- | :--------------- | :---------------------- | :--------------------- | :---------------- |
| Flight time (ms) | 150 ± 50         | 50 ± 5                  | 120 ± 20               | 140 ± 35          |
| Dwell time (ms)  | 100 ± 30         | 30 ± 3                  | 80 ± 15                | 95 ± 22           |
| Typing rhythm CV | 0.30 ± 0.15      | 0.04 ± 0.02             | 0.16 ± 0.08            | 0.24 ± 0.12       |

Detection thresholds reported in the literature: flight times \< 70ms with CV \< 0.15 strongly indicate scripted bot behavior; dwell times \< 40ms with σ \< 5ms indicate automation.

#### Mouse Movement Analysis {#mouse-movement-analysis}

Mouse dynamics provide rich spatial and kinematic signals. Key results from the literature include:

- **BeCAPTCHA-Mouse:** Neuromotor-feature approach detected high-realism bot trajectories with approximately 93% average accuracy using a single trajectory.
- **Spatial-kinematic CNN:** Reported approximately 96.2% detection accuracy.
- **Time-series sequence learning:** Reported accuracies up to approximately 99.78% on specific datasets.

Key discriminative mouse features include trajectory curvature, speed/acceleration profiles, and micro-hesitations.

#### Session-Level Profiling and Industry Reports {#session-level-profiling-and-industry-reports}

Industry reports (Imperva Bad Bot Report, 2023; Cloudflare Radar, 2023\) document that approximately 70% of bad bots originate from datacenter IPs, scripted bots sustain request rates \> 3 req/s (vs. human \~0.45 req/s), and bot click frequencies reach 3–4× human rates (\~60 clicks/min vs. \~16 clicks/min). Browser fingerprinting and device intelligence are related detection signals but fall outside the scope of this work.

#### Limitations {#limitations}

Behavioral biometrics face some key limitations regarding technical tools and data quality related, which includes noisy or unstable signals associated with sensor limitations and behavioral factors. Additionally, technology advances have led to very well designed adversarial systems that can easily mimic human patterns. All these, alongside limited, biased datasets and privacy concerns, make it difficult to estimate real-world reliability.

Specifically, behavioral bot detection face additional limitations regarding the methodological and modeling choices, which include:

- **Overfitting to specific bot types or datasets:** Models are often trained on limited, biased datasets, which can fail to detect new bot styles, languages, or domains, revealing poor generalization skills.
- **High false positive or negative risk:** Some tools show unstable scores over time, studies have found that even conservative thresholds can misclassify humans as bots or vice versa.
- **Evasive and adaptive bots:** Modern bots are becoming increasingly sophisticated and can adapt to detection mechanisms, imitating human temporal patterns, emotional tone, and coordination behaviors, making it difficult to maintain consistent detection rates.

### 3.3 Combined Approaches {#3.3-combined-approaches}

The literature increasingly recognizes that no single signal type provides sufficient robustness against sophisticated adversaries. Due to these new challenges, most studies and industry efforts are leaning towards developing combined systems integrating transactional behaviors, device fingerprints, network patterns, users specific behaviors, and other contextual signals.

#### Multi-Modal Detection {#multi-modal-detection}

Multi-signal fusion increases robustness because evading one signal typically leaves traces in others. Production systems implement multi-stage detection architectures:

1. **Rule-based filters** (\< 1ms latency): Catch obvious fraud (impossible travel, velocity checks, blacklists), covering approximately 5–10% of fraud.
2. **ML classifiers** (\< 10ms latency): Score all transactions using behavioral \+ transaction features, covering approximately 60–70% of fraud.
3. **Deep learning models** (\< 100ms latency): Analyze complex behavioral sequences for sophisticated attacks, covering approximately 10–20% of fraud.
4. **Manual review** (minutes to hours): Human judgment for ambiguous cases with analyst feedback loops.

#### Feature Fusion vs. Model Fusion {#feature-fusion-vs.-model-fusion}

Two strategies exist for combining signal types. Feature fusion concatenates behavioral and transactional features into a single feature vector for a unified model, this is the approach adopted in this work. Model fusion trains separate specialized models for each signal type and combines their outputs through ensemble averaging, stacking, or cascading. Feature fusion is simpler to implement and allows the model to learn cross-domain interactions, while model fusion provides modularity and allows independent retraining.

#### Research Gap {#research-gap}

While both behavioral bot detection and transactional fraud detection are well-studied individually, limited work systematically combines both signal types within a single supervised pipeline with rigorous evaluation. Most systems either focus on transaction-level features or on behavioral biometrics, rarely both in the same model with a controlled comparison of feature set contributions. This dissertation addresses this gap by evaluating behavioral-only, transactional-only, and combined feature sets across multiple algorithms with statistical robustness.

### 3.4 Ensemble Methods for Fraud Detection {#3.4-ensemble-methods-for-fraud-detection}

Ensemble methods have become the dominant approach in fraud detection due to their ability to handle class imbalance, capture non-linear relationships, and provide robust performance across diverse datasets (He & Garcia, 2009; Breiman, 2001).

#### Random Forest {#random-forest}

Random Forests combine multiple bagged decision trees to reduce variance and improve generalization (Breiman, 2001). For fraud detection, their key strengths include built-in feature importance ranking, robustness to overfitting on noisy fraud labels, ability to handle mixed feature types without extensive preprocessing, and class-weighted loss functions to address imbalance. Comparative studies consistently rank Random Forests among the top performers on tabular fraud datasets, sometimes achieving the best overall performance or second only to boosting methods.

#### Gradient Boosting {#gradient-boosting}

Gradient Boosting machines (GBM, XGBoost, LightGBM) sequentially train weak learners that correct errors from previous iterations, focusing optimization on hard-to-classify examples (Friedman, 2001). XGBoost introduced regularization and scalability improvements (Chen & Guestrin, 2016), while LightGBM further optimized training speed through gradient-based one-side sampling and histogram binning (Ke et al., 2017). This makes them particularly effective for fraud detection where the minority class contains diverse sub-patterns. Gradient Boosting has been reported as the leading approach in multiple fraud detection competitions and benchmarks, often achieving top accuracy/F1 and robustness on highly imbalanced online payment data.

#### Hybrid Ensemble Strategies {#hybrid-ensemble-strategies}

Hybrid ensemble frameworks that combine sampling techniques with ensemble learning have demonstrated superior performance:

- **EasyEnsemble:** Trains classifiers on different balanced subsets and combines predictions. Leads in ROC-AUC in comparative experiments on imbalanced benchmarks.
- **RUSBoost and SMOTEBoost:** Integrate random undersampling or SMOTE within boosting iterations, improving F1 and AUC compared with single classifiers.
- **Cost-sensitive ensembles:** Combine class-weighted loss functions with ensemble diversity to address imbalance at both data and algorithm levels.

Some studies have found that traditional oversampling combined with ensembles can rival or beat GAN-based augmentation while having much lower processing costs (Khan et al., 2023; Elreedy et al., 2024).

#### Baseline Models {#baseline-models}

Baseline models provide essential reference points for evaluating ensemble performance:

- **DummyClassifier (class prior):** Predicts based solely on class distribution, establishing the minimum performance any model should exceed. Useful for validating that learned models capture genuine signals.
- **Logistic Regression:** Provides a linear baseline with interpretable coefficients. With class_weight='balanced', it handles imbalance through adjusted loss weights. Logistic Regression is usually outperformed by ensembles on minority-class recall/AUC, but the performance gap quantifies the value of non-linear modeling.

### 3.5 Summary and Research Gap {#3.5-summary-and-research-gap}

The literature review reveals several gaps that motivate this work.

**Gap 1: Limited multi-modal feature fusion.** Most fraud detection research focuses on either transactional features or behavioral biometrics, but rarely combines both signal types within a single supervised pipeline with controlled ablation. While multi-modal detection is recognized as essential for robust fraud prevention (Akoglu et al., 2014), systematic comparisons of individual vs. combined feature set contributions across multiple algorithms remain scarce. Most studies that do combine signals lack controlled experiments isolating the marginal contribution of each feature set.

**Gap 2: Threshold selection methodology.** Threshold selection methodology in many published works suffers from data leakage, selecting the operating threshold on the test set rather than on a separate validation set, which produces optimistically biased performance estimates (Elkan, 2001). The literature emphasizes that thresholds must be selected on representative validation data mirroring production prevalence, yet many studies do not explicitly report this separation. Furthermore, different threshold optimization strategies (F1-maximization, cost-sensitive, precision-constrained) are rarely compared systematically.

**Gap 3: Statistical robustness.** Many studies report results from a single random split, making it difficult to assess whether findings are artifacts of a particular partition. Multi-seed evaluation with mean ± standard deviation reporting remains uncommon in fraud detection research, despite being standard practice in other machine learning domains (Aguiar et al., 2022). This limits the reproducibility and generalizability of reported results.

**Gap 4: Imbalance handling comparison.** While numerous techniques exist for handling class imbalance, few studies provide controlled comparisons across these strategies using identical experimental setups and evaluation protocols.

This dissertation addresses these gaps by: (1) evaluating behavioral-only, transactional-only, and combined feature sets to quantify the value of feature fusion; (2) using strict train/validation/test separation with threshold tuning exclusively on the validation set; (3) running all experiments across five random seeds for statistical robustness; and (4) comparing four algorithms (DummyClassifier, Logistic Regression, Random Forest, Gradient Boosting) to provide a comprehensive baseline comparison. Additionally, a precision-constraint sweep (0.40–0.80) is conducted to analyze the recall-precision tradeoff across different operating points, informing practical deployment decisions.

---

## 4\. Approach {#4.-approach}

In this section we will present the overall research and technical approach used to design, implement, and evaluate fraud detection models using both behavioral and transactional features. The methodology follows a data-centric and experimental paradigm, where synthetic data generation, feature engineering, and supervised machine learning are employed to systematically compare different algorithmic approaches and feature combinations.

The core objective of this approach is to assess whether augmenting traditional transactional fraud detection with behavioral features improves model performance, particularly in terms of precision and recall, while maintaining computational efficiency and interpretability, under a realistic operational constraint. This constraint is defined by the need to maintain a high precision rate, which is critical for minimizing false positives that could lead to customer friction and operational overhead. To achieve this, the system is designed as a modular pipeline that allows controlled experimentation with different feature combinations, algorithms, and evaluation strategies.

The methodology is structured around a series of controlled experiments that systematically vary the feature set, algorithm, and evaluation criteria to isolate the impact of behavioral features on fraud detection performance. From a design perspective, the approach reflects principles commonly used in modern data-driven systems, emphasizing scalability, reproducibility, and modularity. These principles are aligned with real-world fintech architectures, where fraud detection systems must operate under strict performance and reliability constraints.

The methodology follows a data-centric and experimental paradigm, where synthetic data generation, feature engineering, and supervised machine learning are employed to systematically compare different algorithmic approaches and feature combinations. This approach is particularly suitable for fraud detection research, as it allows for controlled experimentation with varying levels of data complexity and feature richness. The structure is composed of several key components:

- **Data generation:** Synthetic data is generated to simulate realistic user behavior and transaction patterns, calibrated from real-world data sources such as PaySim.
- **Feature engineering:** Derived features are created from the raw data to capture meaningful patterns and relationships that may not be apparent in the original features.
- **Model training:** Multiple machine learning algorithms are trained on the engineered features to compare their performance and identify the most effective approach.
- **Threshold tuning:** The decision threshold is adjusted on a validation set to optimize performance under the precision constraint.
- **Multi-seed aggregation:** Results are averaged across multiple random seeds to ensure statistical robustness.
- **Final evaluation:** The final model is evaluated on a held-out test set to assess its generalization performance.

### 4.1 Overview {#4.1-overview}

The overall system is implemented as an end-to-end pipeline that includes data generation, feature engineering, model training, threshold tuning, and final evaluation. This transforms the raw simulated user activity data into evaluated fraud detection models. The pipeline is composed of six sequential stages, each contributing to the final fraud detection model.

1. **Data generation:** Synthetic datasets are generated by combining transactional data, based on the PaySim calibration, with behavioral data to simulate realistic user activity patterns. The behavioral data is generated using a combination of rule-based and machine learning-based approaches. The combined dataset generation process is parameterized to reflect realistic distributions and fraud patterns.
2. **Feature engineering:** Raw features are transformed into structured inputs for machine learning models. This includes the creation of derived features such as velocity, frequency, and behavioral patterns, encoding categorical variables, and scaling numerical features.
3. **Model training:** Four different algorithms are trained on the engineered features to compare their performance and identify the most effective approach. The algorithms, providing different levels of complexity and interpretability, are DummyClassifier, Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and Gradient Boosting.
4. **Threshold tuning:** Model outputs are probabilistic and need to be converted to binary predictions. The decision threshold is adjusted on a validation set to optimize performance under the precision constraint.
5. **Final evaluation:** The selected models are evaluated on a held-out test set to ensure unbiased assessment of their performance. Metrics such as ROC-AUC, PR-AUC, precision, recall, and F1-score are reported.
6. **Multi-seed aggregation:** To ensure statistical robustness, the entire pipeline is executed across multiple random seeds. The results are then averaged to demonstrate the consistency and reliability of the findings.

Overall, this pipeline demonstrates the effectiveness of synthetic data generation for fraud detection research, providing a controlled environment to test and compare different machine learning approaches while maintaining the realism of the underlying data distribution. The robustness of this approach is further validated through distributional sensitivity analysis (Section 5.2.4), which demonstrates model performance stability across significant improvements in synthetic data fidelity.

### 4.2 Synthetic Data Generation {#4.2-synthetic-data-generation}

#### 4.2.1 PaySim Calibration {#4.2.1-paysim-calibration}

The synthetic data generation pipeline is anchored to the PaySim dataset (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016), a widely used benchmark containing approximately 6.3 million mobile money transactions across 11 features with a fraud rate of \~0.13%. The `paysim_dataset.py` module extracts the following calibration parameters from the raw PaySim data:

- **Transaction type probabilities:** The empirical distribution over PAYMENT, TRANSFER, CASH_OUT, and DEBIT types, used as the base sampling distribution for legitimate users.
- **Log-normal amount parameters:** Mean and standard deviation of log-transformed transaction amounts, computed per transaction type, defining the shape of the amount distribution.
- **Log-normal balance parameters:** Mean and standard deviation of log-transformed origin and destination balances, ensuring realistic account states.
- **Fraud base rate:** \~0.13%, applied as the starting probability in the additive fraud label model.
- **Empirical drain statistics:** 97.6% of fraudulent TRANSFER transactions in PaySim drain the origin account to zero; 50.9% of fraudulent transactions show no change in the destination balance. These statistics serve as calibration targets for the `is_full_drain` and `dest_balance_unchanged` derived features.

The PaySim CSV headers (`step, type, amount, nameOrig, oldbalanceOrg, newbalanceOrig, nameDest, oldbalanceDest, newbalanceDest, isFraud, isFlaggedFraud`) are mapped to the synthetic schema during parameter extraction. By anchoring the simulation to these real-world-inspired distributions, the generated dataset maintains realistic patterns while enabling controlled experimentation.

#### 4.2.2 Behavioral Feature Generation {#4.2.2-behavioral-feature-generation}

Behavioral features are generated independently by `behavioral_dataset.py` to simulate user interaction patterns. Four profile types were defined, each with distinct distributional parameters grounded in the behavioral biometrics and bot detection literature:

| Profile      | Proportion | Description                                                  |
| :----------- | :--------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- |
| human_normal | 72.25%     | Typical users with natural behavioral variability            |
| human_fast   | 12.75%     | Power users with faster but still human interaction patterns |
| bot_standard | 10.50%     | Scripted bots exhibiting machine-speed interactions          |
| bot_stealth  | 4.50%      | Sophisticated bots designed to mimic human behavior          |

Five continuous behavioral features and two binary network indicators are generated per profile using the following distributions:

| Feature                  | human_normal  | human_fast    | bot_standard   | bot_stealth   | Source                |
| :----------------------- | :------------ | :------------ | :------------- | :------------ | :-------------------- |
| keystroke_flight_time_ms | N(150, 50\)   | N(150, 50\)   | N(50, 5\)      | N(125, 35\)   | Industry observations |
| session_duration_sec     | Exp(λ=1/120)  | Exp(λ=1/55)   | Exp(λ=1/6)     | Exp(λ=1/75)   | Industry observations |
| checkout_velocity_sec    | N(3.5, 1.2)   | N(2.0, 0.8)   | N(0.3, 0.1)    | N(2.0, 0.7)   | Industry observations |
| mouse_speed_variance     | N(0.35, 0.15) | N(0.40, 0.12) | N(0.02, 0.005) | N(0.20, 0.08) | Industry observations |
| pages_visited            | Poisson(8)    | Poisson(12)   | Poisson(3)     | Poisson(6)    | Session profiling     |
| is_datacenter_ip         | P=0.00        | P=0.02        | P=0.70         | P=0.30        | Industry observations |
| impossible_travel_flag   | P=0.02        | P=0.03        | P=0.60         | P=0.25        | Industry reports      |

Key design decisions:

- **Stealth bot parameters** are deliberately positioned between standard bot and human distributions to create a challenging detection scenario. For example, stealth keystroke flight time (125 ± 35 ms) overlaps substantially with the human distribution (150 ± 50 ms), while standard bots (50 ± 5 ms) are easily separable.
- **Network indicators** reflect industry findings that \~70% of bad bots originate from datacenter IPs, while stealth bots increasingly use residential proxies (modeled as 30% datacenter rate).
- **Noise injection:** 3% label noise and 5% Gaussian measurement noise are added to simulate real-world imperfections in data collection.

This design allows models to learn patterns associated with automation without explicitly encoding the fraud label, while ensuring that stealth bots present a realistic detection challenge.

#### 4.2.3 Transactional Feature Generation {#4.2.3-transactional-feature-generation}

Transactional features are generated by `fraud_dataset.py` using PaySim-calibrated distributions and profile-specific adjustments:

**Transaction type selection.** Each transaction's type is sampled from the empirical PaySim distribution. For bot profiles, the base probabilities are adjusted with multipliers to reflect the literature finding that fraud concentrates in high-value transfer types (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016):

| Transaction Type | Human Multiplier | Bot Multiplier | Effect                                         |
| :--------------- | :--------------- | :------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| TRANSFER         | 1.0×             | 2.2×           | Bots favor transfers (fraud-prone)             |
| CASH_OUT         | 1.0×             | 2.0×           | Bots favor cash-outs (monetization)            |
| PAYMENT          | 1.0×             | 0.3×           | Bots rarely make payments (no fraud in PaySim) |
| DEBIT            | 1.0×             | 1.0×           | Unchanged                                      |

Human profiles can still generate TRANSFER and CASH_OUT transactions, preserving realism and avoiding deterministic rules that would trivialize the classification task.

**Amount generation.** Transaction amounts are sampled from a log-normal distribution parameterized by PaySim's empirical mean and standard deviation in log-space. Bot transactions receive a \+0.5 shift in the log-space mean, producing systematically larger amounts that align with the finding that fraudulent transactions target higher values.

**Balance generation and capping.** Origin and destination balances are sampled from PaySim-calibrated log-normal distributions. To prevent unrealistic scenarios where the transaction amount exceeds the available balance, amounts are capped to a random fraction (5–85%) of the origin balance. This capping creates a realistic distribution of drain ratios while ensuring that full-drain events (`balance_drain_ratio ≈ 1.0`) occur predominantly in bot/fraud scenarios.

**Risky pattern injection.** The `apply_risky_patterns` function probabilistically introduces two fraud-indicative patterns:

- **Full drain:** For bot profiles with TRANSFER or CASH_OUT types, the new balance is set to zero with elevated probability, mimicking the PaySim observation that 97.6% of fraudulent transfers drain the account.
- **Destination unchanged:** The destination balance remains unchanged after receiving funds, a pattern observed in \~50.9% of PaySim fraud.

**Derived features.** Three features are engineered from raw balance fields to make the fraud signal more accessible to classifiers:

- `balance_drain_ratio = (old_balance_origin − new_balance_origin) / (old_balance_origin + 1)` — captures the proportion of the account drained.
- `is_full_drain = 1 if new_balance_origin == 0 and old_balance_origin > 0` — binary indicator of complete account drainage.
- `dest_balance_unchanged = 1 if old_balance_dest == new_balance_dest` — binary indicator of the destination anomaly.

#### 4.2.4 Fraud Label Assignment {#4.2.4-fraud-label-assignment}

Fraud labels are assigned probabilistically by the `assign_fraud_label` function using an additive probability model. Each transaction starts with the PaySim base fraud rate (\~0.13%), and independent risk factors incrementally increase the fraud probability:

**Table 4.1: Additive Fraud Probability Model**

| Risk Factor                | Condition                    | Probability Boost   |
| :------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------ |
| Bot profile (any)          | profile starts with "bot\_"  | \+0.20              |
| Bot standard               | profile \== "bot_standard"   | \+0.05 (additional) |
| Bot stealth                | profile \== "bot_stealth"    | \+0.02 (additional) |
| High-risk transaction type | type ∈ {TRANSFER, CASH_OUT}  | \+0.18              |
| Full account drain         | is_full_drain \== 1          | \+0.22              |
| Destination unchanged      | dest_balance_unchanged \== 1 | \+0.10              |
| Large amount               | amount \> 200,000            | \+0.08              |
| Datacenter IP              | is_datacenter_ip \== 1       | \+0.05              |
| Impossible travel          | impossible_travel_flag \== 1 | \+0.08              |

The final fraud probability is capped at 0.95 and a Bernoulli draw determines the binary label. This produces a realistic fraud distribution where standard bots performing high-value transfers with full drains have the highest fraud probability (\~0.78), while legitimate humans making payments have near-baseline rates (\~0.13%).

**Design choice — avoiding circular leakage.** The risky patterns (drain, destination-unchanged) are injected by `apply_risky_patterns` _before_ the fraud label is assigned, and they are applied probabilistically based on profile type rather than on the fraud label itself. This ensures that while drain patterns are more common among bots (and hence correlated with fraud), the causal direction flows from profile → risky pattern → fraud probability, not from fraud label → pattern. Models must therefore learn to detect fraud through the combination of observable features rather than through a deterministic mapping.

#### 4.2.5 Distribution Validation {#4.2.5-distribution-validation}

To ensure realism, the synthetic dataset is validated against the original PaySim dataset using statistical tests and visualizations implemented in `validate_distributions.py`. The validation process includes:

- **Two-sample Kolmogorov–Smirnov (KS) tests** on log1p-transformed amounts and balances. The KS test evaluates the null hypothesis that the synthetic and PaySim samples are drawn from the same continuous distribution; a low KS statistic (close to 0\) and high p-value (\> 0.05) indicate strong alignment.
- **Quantile-quantile (QQ) plots** comparing sorted quantiles of PaySim and synthetic amounts to visually assess distributional alignment. Points falling on the 45° diagonal indicate identical distributions.
- **Transaction type distribution comparison** via side-by-side bar charts, verifying that the synthetic type proportions match PaySim's empirical distribution for the majority (human) population.
- **Fraud rate comparison** by transaction type, ensuring that synthetic fraud rates are concentrated in TRANSFER and CASH_OUT types consistent with PaySim.
- **Feature correlation matrix** heatmap showing pairwise Pearson correlations among all numeric features, verifying that behavioral and transactional features are largely uncorrelated (confirming non-redundant signal types) and that expected associations (e.g., is_bot ↔ is_datacenter_ip) are present.

These validations confirm that the generated data preserves key statistical properties of the reference dataset while allowing controlled variation for experimentation. Detailed results are presented in Section 5.2.

### 4.3 Feature Engineering {#4.3-feature-engineering}

The feature engineering stage transforms raw synthetic data into structured model inputs. Three feature sets are defined to enable controlled ablation of behavioral versus transactional signal:

**Behavioral feature set (7 features, target: is_bot):**

| Feature                  | Type       | Description                              |
| :----------------------- | :--------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| session_duration_sec     | Continuous | Total session time                       |
| checkout_velocity_sec    | Continuous | Speed of checkout flow                   |
| mouse_speed_variance     | Continuous | Variance in mouse movement speed         |
| keystroke_flight_time_ms | Continuous | Inter-key interval                       |
| pages_visited            | Discrete   | Number of pages viewed                   |
| is_datacenter_ip         | Binary     | IP originates from a datacenter          |
| impossible_travel_flag   | Binary     | Geographically impossible login sequence |

**Transactional feature set (9 features, target: is_fraud):**

| Feature                | Type        | Description                                          |
| :--------------------- | :---------- | :--------------------------------------------------- |
| transaction_type       | Categorical | One-hot encoded (PAYMENT, TRANSFER, CASH_OUT, DEBIT) |
| amount                 | Continuous  | Transaction value                                    |
| old_balance_origin     | Continuous  | Origin balance before transaction                    |
| new_balance_origin     | Continuous  | Origin balance after transaction                     |
| old_balance_dest       | Continuous  | Destination balance before transaction               |
| new_balance_dest       | Continuous  | Destination balance after transaction                |
| balance_drain_ratio    | Continuous  | Derived: proportion of origin account drained        |
| is_full_drain          | Binary      | Derived: origin balance reduced to zero              |
| dest_balance_unchanged | Binary      | Derived: destination balance did not increase        |

**Combined feature set (16 features, target: is_fraud):** The union of all behavioral and transactional features.

**Encoding.** The categorical variable `transaction_type` is one-hot encoded using scikit-learn's `OneHotEncoder` with `drop="first"` to avoid multicollinearity. The encoder is fit exclusively on the training set and applied to validation and test sets to prevent data leakage. Tree-based models (Random Forest, Gradient Boosting) do not require feature scaling. For Logistic Regression, `class_weight="balanced"` adjusts the loss function to account for class imbalance, and the solver handles the mixed feature scales internally.

The `profile` column (human_normal, human_fast, bot_standard, bot_stealth) is retained in the dataframe for post-hoc analysis (e.g., stealth bot recall) but is explicitly excluded from model inputs to prevent leakage of the synthetic generation logic.

### 4.4 Model Training Pipeline {#4.4-model-training-pipeline}

#### 4.4.1 Data Splitting Strategy {#4.4.1-data-splitting-strategy}

The dataset was split into three subsets to ensure robust model evaluation and prevent data leakage:

- **Training set (70%):** Used for model training and hyperparameter tuning
- **Validation set (10%):** Used for model selection and hyperparameter optimization
- **Test set (20%):** Used for final model evaluation to provide an unbiased estimate of performance

The splits were stratified to maintain the same proportion of fraudulent and non-fraudulent transactions across all three sets. This ensures that the model is evaluated on data that reflects the true distribution of the problem. The behavioral profile column is retained for analysis but excluded from model inputs to prevent leakage of synthetic generation logic.

#### 4.4.2 Algorithm Selection {#4.4.2-algorithm-selection}

For algorithms were selected to represent different modelling paradigms:

- **Dummy Classifier:** Serves as a baseline, using class priors.
- **Logistic Regression:** Linear model with `class_weight='balanced'` to handle class imbalance.
- **Random Forest:** Ensemble method using bagging. Hyperparamethers were tuned using GridSearchCV with PR-AUC as the optimization metric.
- **Gradient Boosting:** Boosting-based ensemble model that sequentially corrects errors. Hyperparamethers were tuned using GridSearchCV with PR-AUC as the optimization metric.

The use of PR-AUC as the tuning metric reflects the importance of performance on imbalanced datasets.

#### 4.4.3 Threshold Tuning {#4.4.3-threshold-tuning}

The threshold tuning process ensures that the models are evaluated under the same precision constraint, allowing for a fair comparison of their recall performance. The threshold is selected using the validation set, not the test set, to avoid optimistic bias.

The selection criterion is to maximize recall subject to maintaining a minimum precision constraint. The initial constraint of precision ≥ 0.90 was found to be too severe for most fraud detection models (Section 5.3.1), motivating a systematic precision-constraint sweep (Section 5.3.2) that identified precision ≥ 0.50 as the optimal operating point. This reflects real-world constraints in fraud detection, where minimizing false positives must be balanced against acceptable recall. The selected threshold is then fixed and applied to the test set for final evaluation.

#### 4.4.4 Statistical Robustness {#4.4.4-statistical-robustness}

The statistical robustness analysis ensures that the results are not artifacts of a single random split. By using 5 random seeds (42, 123, 456, 789, 2024), the dataset is generated independently for each seed, and the results are aggregated to provide mean ± standard deviation for all metrics. This approach helps to validate the consistency and reliability of the findings.

### 4.5 Tools and Environment {#4.5-tools-and-environment}

The tools and environment required for this project include the following:

- Python 3.x
- scikit-learn
- pandas
- numpy
- matplotlib
- tabulate
- Faker

## 5\. Evaluation and Results {#5.-evaluation-and-results}

### 5.1 Experimental Setup {#5.1-experimental-setup}

This section describes the complete experimental configuration, including dataset characteristics, algorithm selection, evaluation metrics, and the statistical validation protocol. All parameters were aligned with values reported in the literature review (Sections 2 and 3\) prior to running experiments.

#### 5.1.1 Dataset {#5.1.1-dataset}

Each experiment uses 10,000 synthetic transactions generated by the combined pipeline (`fraud_dataset.py`), which merges behavioral features from `behavioral_dataset.py` with transactional features from the PaySim dataset (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016), which were calibrated in `paysim_dataset.py`. A new dataset is independently generated for each of the five random seeds to ensure that results are not artifacts of a single data partition.

**User profiles and composition:**

| Profile      | Type       | Proportion | Description                                        |
| :----------- | :--------- | :--------- | :------------------------------------------------- |
| human_normal | Legitimate | 72.25%     | Standard users with natural behavioral variability |
| human_fast   | Legitimate | 12.75%     | Power users with faster interaction patterns       |
| bot_standard | Malicious  | 10.50%     | Scripted bots with machine-speed interactions      |
| bot_stealth  | Malicious  | 4.50%      | Sophisticated bots mimicking human behavior        |

**Behavioral feature parameters (literature-aligned):**

| Feature                           | human_normal | human_fast | bot_standard | bot_stealth | Literature Source     |
| :-------------------------------- | :----------- | :--------- | :----------- | :---------- | :-------------------- |
| keystroke_flight_time_ms (mean±σ) | 150 ± 50     | 150 ± 50   | 50 ± 5       | 125 ± 35    | Industry observations |
| session_duration_sec (median)     | \~120s       | \~55s      | \~6s         | \~75s       | Industry observations |
| is_datacenter_ip (rate)           | 0%           | 2%         | 70%          | 30%         | Industry observations |
| impossible_travel_flag (rate)     | 2%           | 3%         | 60%          | 25%         | Industry reports      |

**Transactional feature parameters (PaySim-calibrated):**

| Parameter                     | Value                 | Source                                |
| :---------------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------------------------ |
| Fraud base rate               | \~0.13% (from PaySim) | Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016              |
| Bot TRANSFER multiplier       | 2.2×                  | PaySim fraud-only-in-TRANSFER pattern |
| Bot CASH_OUT multiplier       | 2.0×                  | PaySim fraud-only-in-CASH_OUT pattern |
| Bot PAYMENT multiplier        | 0.3×                  | PaySim: no fraud in PAYMENT           |
| TRANSFER/CASH_OUT fraud boost | \+0.18                | PaySim conditional fraud rate         |
| is_full_drain fraud boost     | \+0.22                | 97.6% drain rate in PaySim fraud      |

**Three feature sets** are evaluated to answer the research questions:

| Feature Set   | Target   | \# Features | Features                                                                                                                                                                           |
| :------------ | :------- | :---------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Behavioral    | is_bot   | 7           | session_duration_sec, checkout_velocity_sec, mouse_speed_variance, keystroke_flight_time_ms, impossible_travel_flag, is_datacenter_ip, pages_visited                               |
| Transactional | is_fraud | 9           | transaction_type (one-hot encoded), amount, old_balance_origin, new_balance_origin, old_balance_dest, new_balance_dest, balance_drain_ratio, is_full_drain, dest_balance_unchanged |
| Combined      | is_fraud | 16          | All behavioral \+ transactional features                                                                                                                                           |

Here it is important to note that, while Transactional and Combined datasets use the `is_fraud` target, Behavioral uses `is_bot` as the purpose of these set of features is to demonstrate that the algorithm can correctly identify humans from bots, based on these selected features.
