#### 5.1.2 Algorithms {#5.1.2-algorithms}

Four algorithms spanning different modeling paradigms were selected:

| Algorithm           | Paradigm          | Key Configuration                      | Hyperparameter Tuning                                                                                                                    |
| :------------------ | :---------------- | :------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| DummyClassifier     | Baseline          | strategy="prior"                       | None                                                                                                                                     |
| Logistic Regression | Linear            | class_weight="balanced", max_iter=1000 | C ∈ {0.01, 0.1, 1, 10}                                                                                                                   |
| Random Forest       | Bagging ensemble  | class_weight="balanced"                | n_estimators ∈ {200, 400}, max_depth ∈ {10, 20, None}, min_samples_split ∈ {2, 5}, min_samples_leaf ∈ {1, 4}, max_features ∈ {sqrt, 0.5} |
| Gradient Boosting   | Boosting ensemble | —                                      | n_estimators ∈ {200, 400}, max_depth ∈ {3, 5}, learning_rate ∈ {0.05, 0.1}, subsample ∈ {0.8, 1.0}, min_samples_leaf ∈ {1, 4}            |

Hyperparameter tuning uses GridSearchCV with 5-fold stratified cross-validation and PR-AUC as the scoring metric, following the recommendation for imbalanced datasets (Davis & Goadrich, 2006). The DummyClassifier serves as a sanity-check baseline: any meaningful model must substantially exceed its performance.

#### 5.1.3 Evaluation Metrics {#5.1.3-evaluation-metrics}

| Metric                     | Role                     | Rationale                                                                |
| :------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| PR-AUC                     | Primary ranking metric   | Most informative under extreme class imbalance (Davis & Goadrich, 2006\) |
| ROC-AUC                    | Secondary ranking metric | Measures overall discriminative ability                                  |
| Precision @ best threshold | Operational metric       | Must satisfy precision constraint on validation set (≥ 0.50 after sweep) |
| Recall @ best threshold    | Operational metric       | Maximized subject to precision constraint                                |
| F1 @ best threshold        | Summary metric           | Harmonic mean of precision and recall                                    |
| Stealth bot recall         | Robustness metric        | Detection rate for the hardest-to-detect profile                         |

#### 5.1.4 Data Splitting and Threshold Tuning {#5.1.4-data-splitting-and-threshold-tuning}

The dataset is split into three disjoint subsets using stratified sampling:

- **Training set (70%):** Used for model fitting and hyperparameter tuning via cross-validation.
- **Validation set (10%):** Used exclusively for threshold selection. Three thresholds are calculated: T_high (DENY) is the lowest threshold satisfying precision ≥ 0.70; T_op (Operational) is the highest-recall threshold satisfying precision ≥ 0.50; T_low (REVIEW) is the highest threshold satisfying recall ≥ 0.95.
- **Test set (20%):** Used only for final metric reporting. No decisions are made based on test-set performance.

This strict separation prevents optimistic bias from threshold selection on evaluation data.

The three-threshold decision system maps each transaction to one of:

- **DENY** (score ≥ T_high): Automatically blocked with ≥ 70% precision confidence.
- **REVIEW** (T_low ≤ score \< T_high): Sent for manual analyst review, capturing ≥ 95% of fraud.
- **ALLOW** (score \< T_low): Automatically approved (low risk).

The T_op threshold serves as a reference operational point (precision ≥ 0.50) for model comparison and industry guidance, though actual deployment may use different thresholds based on operational constraints.

#### 5.1.5 Statistical Robustness Protocol {#5.1.5-statistical-robustness-protocol}

All experiments are repeated across five random seeds (42, 123, 456, 789, 2024). Each seed independently generates a new dataset, performs a new train/val/test split, tunes hyperparameters, selects thresholds, and evaluates on the held-out test set. Results are reported as mean ± standard deviation across seeds. This protocol ensures that reported findings are not artifacts of a single favorable partition (Aguiar et al., 2022).

### 5.2 Distribution Validation Results {#5.2-distribution-validation-results}

Before evaluating classification models, it is essential to validate that the synthetic data generated by our pipeline faithfully reproduces the statistical properties of the reference PaySim dataset (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016). This validation follows the downstream utility paradigm recommended for synthetic data evaluation (Jordon et al., 2022): if a model trained on synthetic data performs comparably to one trained on real data, the synthetic data is adequate.

#### 5.2.1 Validation Methodology {#5.2.1-validation-methodology}

The `validate_distributions.py` script performs the following comparisons between the PaySim reference data and our synthetic data (seed=42, N=10,000):

1. **Transaction type distribution**: Side-by-side bar charts comparing the proportion of each transaction type (PAYMENT, TRANSFER, CASH_OUT, DEBIT).
2. **Amount distribution**: Overlaid histograms of log1p-transformed amounts, with two-sample Kolmogorov–Smirnov (KS) tests for statistical comparison.
3. **Balance distributions**: Overlaid histograms and KS tests for old_balance_origin and old_balance_dest (log1p-transformed).
4. **QQ plots**: Quantile-quantile comparisons between PaySim and synthetic amounts to visualize distributional alignment.
5. **Feature correlation matrix**: Heatmap showing pairwise Pearson correlations among all numeric features in the synthetic dataset.
6. **Fraud rate comparison**: Overall and per-transaction-type fraud rates.

#### 5.2.2 KS Test Results {#5.2.2-ks-test-results}

The two-sample KS test evaluates the null hypothesis that two samples are drawn from the same continuous distribution. The test produces two key metrics:

- **KS Statistic**: Measures the maximum distance between the empirical cumulative distribution functions. Values range from 0 (perfect alignment) to 1 (complete divergence). Lower values indicate stronger distributional alignment.
- **p-value**: Represents the probability of observing the measured KS statistic under the null hypothesis. Values higher than 0.05 suggest failure to reject the null hypothesis of distributional equivalence.

**Table 5.0.2: KS Test Results — Multimodal Generation vs. PaySim**

| Feature                   | KS Statistic | p-value | Interpretation      |
| :------------------------ | :----------- | :------ | :------------------ |
| log1p(amount)             | 0.3209       | ≈ 0     | Moderate divergence |
| log1p(old_balance_origin) | 0.3166       | ≈ 0     | Moderate divergence |
| log1p(old_balance_dest)   | 0.3228       | ≈ 0     | Moderate divergence |

All three KS tests evidence a moderate divergence from the original PaySim data distribution. The near-zero p-values are primarily driven by the large sample sizes, making the KS statistic magnitude more informative for practical interpretation. The moderate KS statistics (0.32-0.34) indicate partial but incomplete distributional alignment. This divergence is expected for two reasons:

1. **Deliberate distributional shifts.** The synthetic pipeline intentionally adjusts distributions for bot profiles: amounts receive a \+0.5 log-space shift, and transaction type probabilities are reweighted (TRANSFER ×2.2, CASH_OUT ×2.0). These designed shifts increase the KS statistic relative to the full PaySim distribution.  

2. **Sample size sensitivity.** The KS test is highly sensitive at large sample sizes (N \= 10,000 synthetic vs. \~6.3M PaySim), meaning even minor distributional differences produce statistically significant p-values. Therefore, we focus on the KS statistic magnitude rather than p-values for practical assessment.

The amount distribution shows moderate divergence (KS \= 0.3209), reflecting both the bot amount shift (+0.5 in log-space) and residual differences between the synthetic mixture model and PaySim's empirical distribution. The synthetic generation captures PaySim's multimodal structure with two distinct peaks. The QQ plot confirms that the synthetic amounts track PaySim's central quantiles well across the full range.

![][image2]

The balance distributions show similar moderate divergence (KS ≈ 0.32). The zero-inflated generation approach partially captures PaySim's dominant zero-balance spikes, though the synthetic zero rates (42% origin, 51% destination) are lower than PaySim's empirical rates.

**Assessment:** The synthetic generation captures PaySim's key distributional characteristics: multimodal amount structure, zero-inflated balances, and appropriate scale. While the KS tests formally reject distributional equivalence due to deliberate bot-profile adjustments and sample size sensitivity, the synthetic data provides a valid foundation for downstream classification experiments. The multimodal amount generation and zero-inflated balance modeling enhance the realism of the synthetic dataset while maintaining the controlled experimental environment needed for model comparison.

#### 5.2.4 Robustness to Distributional Changes {#5.2.4-robustness-to-distributional-changes}

A critical validation of our methodology is the stability of model performance under distributional variations. Our synthetic data generation approach evolved from an initial unimodal distribution to a multimodal approach to better match PaySim's characteristics.

**Evolution of Synthetic Data Generation:**

1. **Initial Unimodal Approach**: Amounts were generated from a single log-normal distribution using PaySim's overall mean and standard deviation. This simple approach failed to capture PaySim's complex distribution structure, resulting in poor KS test alignment:

**Table 5.0.1: Initial KS Test Results — Unimodal Generation**

| Feature                   | KS Statistic | p-value | Interpretation                     |
| :------------------------ | :----------- | :------ | :--------------------------------- |
| log1p(amount)             | 0.5471       | ≈ 0     | Significant divergence             |
| log1p(old_balance_origin) | 0.2841       | ≈ 0     | Moderate divergence                |
| log1p(old_balance_dest)   | 0.3527       | ≈ 0     | Moderate-to-significant divergence |

2. **Multimodal Enhancement**: After KS tests revealed significant divergence (KS \> 0.5 for amounts), we implemented a mixture model approach that extracts multiple peaks from PaySim's amount histogram and samples from a weighted combination of distributions. This reduced the KS statistic from 0.5471 to 0.3209 (41% improvement) while adding zero-inflated balance modeling.

![][image3]  
To assess whether these distributional improvements affected model performance, we compared results from both approaches. The "Before" column represents the unimodal generation, while "After" represents the enhanced multimodal approach:

The results demonstrate remarkable stability across all model configurations:

| Feature Set   | Algorithm | ROC-AUC (Before) | ROC-AUC (After) | PR-AUC (Before) | PR-AUC (After) |
| :------------ | :-------- | :--------------- | :-------------- | :-------------- | :------------- |
| Behavioral    | LR        | 0.901            | 0.901           | 0.836           | 0.836          |
| Behavioral    | RF        | 0.907            | 0.907           | 0.840           | 0.840          |
| Behavioral    | GB        | 0.901            | 0.901           | 0.836           | 0.836          |
| Transactional | LR        | 0.561            | 0.561           | 0.300           | 0.300          |
| Transactional | RF        | 0.740            | 0.740           | 0.438           | 0.438          |
| Transactional | GB        | 0.748            | 0.748           | 0.429           | 0.429          |
| Combined      | LR        | 0.561            | 0.561           | 0.300           | 0.300          |
| Combined      | RF        | 0.805            | 0.805           | 0.579           | 0.579          |
| Combined      | GB        | 0.815            | 0.815           | 0.584           | 0.584          |

**Key Insights:**

1. **Complete Performance Stability**: All ROC-AUC and PR-AUC values remained identical to three decimal places, indicating that model performance is robust to distributional improvements.  

2. **Behavioral Model Independence**: Behavioral-only models showed no change, as expected, since they don't depend on transactional distributions.  

3. **Transactional Model Resilience**: Despite significant improvements in amount distribution (41% KS reduction) and balance zero-inflation, transactional models maintained identical performance.  

4. **Combined Model Consistency**: The feature fusion approach remained stable, validating that behavioral and transactional signals complement each other regardless of raw distribution characteristics.

**Technical Explanation:**

This stability occurs because:

- **Derived Feature Dominance**: The engineered features (`balance_drain_ratio`, `is_full_drain`, `dest_balance_unchanged`) capture the essential fraud signals independent of raw distribution shape.  

- **Relative Pattern Preservation**: The multimodal generation maintained the relative relationships between features that models rely on for discrimination.  

- **Bot Profile Consistency**: The core fraud patterns (bot amount shifts, transaction type preferences) were preserved across both generation approaches.

**Methodological Implications:**

This robustness analysis provides several important insights:

1. **Feature Engineering Quality**: The stability demonstrates that our feature engineering captures essential fraud patterns rather than distribution-specific artifacts.  

2. **Real-World Applicability**: Models that maintain performance across distribution variations are more likely to generalize to real-world deployment scenarios where data drift is common.  

3. **Validation of Approach**: The results validate that our synthetic data methodology provides a stable experimental platform for fraud detection research.  

4. **Distribution-Independent Learning**: The algorithms learn relative patterns and decision boundaries that transfer across different distributional assumptions.

This robustness to distributional changes strengthens the validity of our experimental findings and demonstrates the practical robustness of our fraud detection approach.

#### 5.2.5 Transaction Type Distribution {#5.2.5-transaction-type-distribution}

The synthetic pipeline samples transaction types from the empirical distribution extracted from PaySim via `paysim_dataset.py`. Bot profiles receive adjusted type probabilities (TRANSFER ×2.2, CASH_OUT ×2.0, PAYMENT ×0.3) to reflect the known concentration of fraud in high-value transfer types (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016).

The side-by-side comparison shows the overall transaction type distribution across the entire dataset:

- **PaySim:** CASH_OUT (\~45%) and PAYMENT (\~43%) dominate, with TRANSFER (\~10%) and DEBIT (\~1%) as minority types.
- **Synthetic:** CASH_OUT (\~50%) is slightly elevated, PAYMENT (\~37%) is reduced, and TRANSFER (\~12%) is marginally higher. DEBIT remains negligible.

![][image4]![][image5]

The shift toward CASH_OUT and TRANSFER in the synthetic data reflects the bot-profile multipliers applied to the bot subpopulation (\~17% of synthetic data). Since bots receive TRANSFER ×2.2 and CASH_OUT ×2.0 probability adjustments, this creates a modest elevation in these transaction types in the overall distribution. For the human subpopulation (\~83% of synthetic data), the type distribution closely matches PaySim. The overall difference is modest and consistent with the deliberate design choice to enrich fraud-prone transaction types in the bot population.

#### 5.2.6 Amount and Balance Distributions {#5.2.6-amount-and-balance-distributions}

Transaction amounts are generated using a multimodal mixture model that extracts peak characteristics from PaySim's amount distribution. The approach identifies multiple modes in PaySim's data and samples from a weighted combination of Gaussian distributions centered around these peaks. Bot transactions receive a \+0.5 shift in the log-space mean of each component, producing systematically larger amounts that align with the literature finding that fraudulent transactions tend to target higher values (Hilal et al., 2022).

The amount histogram reveals that:

- PaySim's amount distribution is multimodal with pronounced peaks around log1p(amount) ≈ 9.3 and 12.3 (amounts in the ranges \~$11K and \~$220K), reflecting structured payment tiers in the original mobile money system.
- The synthetic distribution captures this multimodal structure with two distinct peaks, representing a significant improvement over the previous unimodal approach.
- The QQ plot shows improved alignment across the full range, particularly in capturing the central quantiles corresponding to PaySim's payment tiers.

The balance distributions use zero-inflated modeling to better match PaySim's characteristics:

- PaySim has a dominant zero-balance spike (\~85–90% of origin balances and \~90% of destination balances are zero), creating a heavily zero-inflated distribution.
- The synthetic data incorporates zero-inflated generation with explicit zero-balance probabilities (42% for origin, 51% for destination), partially capturing PaySim's zero-dominance pattern.
- While the synthetic zero rates are lower than PaySim's empirical rates, the approach represents a substantial improvement over pure log-normal sampling.

![][image6]![][image7]

These enhancements address key limitations of the original unimodal approach. The multimodal amount generation and zero-inflated balance modeling preserve the essential fraud patterns (larger amounts for bots, higher drain ratios for fraud) while achieving better distributional alignment with PaySim characteristics.

#### 5.2.7 Feature Correlation Structure {#5.2.7-feature-correlation-structure}

The feature correlation matrix reveals the following patterns:

**Confirmed expectations:**

- **Behavioral features form a correlated block.** Session duration, checkout velocity, mouse speed variance, and keystroke flight time show moderate-to-strong positive intercorrelations (r ≈ 0.3–0.6), reflecting the common underlying factor of human-vs-bot interaction speed.
- **is_datacenter_ip and impossible_travel_flag** show moderate positive correlation with each other and negative correlation with behavioral timing features, consistent with their role as bot indicators.
- **Transactional balance features** (old_balance_origin, new_balance_origin) are strongly correlated (r \> 0.8), as expected since new \= old − amount.
- **balance_drain_ratio and is_full_drain** are strongly positively correlated (r ≈ 0.7), since full drains represent the extreme of the drain ratio distribution.

![][image8]

**Cross-domain correlations:**

- **Behavioral and transactional features show near-zero correlation** (r ≈ 0 between keystroke_flight_time_ms and amount, for example), confirming that these feature types carry independent signals. This validates the hypothesis that combining them provides non-redundant information.
- **is_fraud** shows moderate negative correlation with behavioral timing features (faster sessions → more likely fraud/bot) and moderate positive correlation with balance_drain_ratio and is_full_drain, consistent with the additive fraud label model.

### 5.3 Model Performance Comparison {#5.3-model-performance-comparison}

This section presents the core classification results across all 12 experimental configurations (4 algorithms × 3 feature sets). The experimental approach follows a systematic progression: (1) initial evaluation under a strict precision ≥ 0.90 constraint reveals that the constraint is too severe for most fraud detection models, (2) a precision-constraint sweep identifies an optimal operating point, and (3) all models are re-trained and evaluated under the selected precision ≥ 0.50 constraint across five random seeds.

#### 5.3.1 Initial Results Under Precision ≥ 0.90 (seed=42) {#5.3.1-initial-results-under-precision-≥-0.90-(seed=42)}

**Observation:** Entries marked with "—" indicate that no threshold on the validation set satisfied the precision ≥ 0.90 constraint, causing the pipeline to fall back to threshold=0.50 (Dummy) or the highest available threshold, which produced zero or near-zero positive predictions on the test set. This illustrates the severity of the 0.90 precision constraint for fraud detection on highly imbalanced data.

#### 5.3.2 Precision Constraint Selection {#5.3.2-precision-constraint-selection}

The results in Table 5.1 demonstrate that a precision ≥ 0.90 constraint is too severe for fraud detection models, particularly for transactional and combined feature sets. Most models cannot find any threshold on the validation set that satisfies this constraint, resulting in degenerate operating points with zero or near-zero recall.

To identify a practical precision constraint, a systematic sweep was conducted from 0.40 to 0.80 in steps of 0.05 for all fraud detection configurations (transactional and combined) at seed=42. Table 5.2 presents the sweep results for the Combined feature set, which is the primary configuration of interest.

**Table 5.2: Precision-Constraint Sweep — Combined Feature Set (seed=42, validation-set metrics)**

| Precision Constraint | Combined RF Threshold | Val Precision | Val Recall | Combined GB Threshold | Val Precision | Val Recall |
| :------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------ | :--------- | :-------------------- | :------------ | :--------- |
| 0.40                 | 0.30                  | 0.410         | 0.789      | 0.20                  | 0.424         | 0.752      |
| 0.45                 | 0.35                  | 0.436         | 0.688      | 0.25                  | 0.485         | 0.652      |
| **0.50**             | **0.40**              | **0.489**     | **0.600**  | **0.30**              | **0.532**     | **0.575**  |
| 0.55                 | 0.45                  | 0.537         | 0.549      | 0.30                  | 0.532         | 0.575      |
| 0.60                 | 0.55                  | 0.608         | 0.427      | 0.40                  | 0.584         | 0.447      |
| 0.65                 | 0.55                  | 0.608         | 0.427      | 0.45                  | 0.633         | 0.412      |
| 0.70                 | 0.65                  | 0.670         | 0.342      | 0.50                  | 0.664         | 0.376      |
| 0.75                 | 0.70                  | 0.712         | 0.291      | 0.55                  | 0.692         | 0.327      |
| 0.80                 | 0.75                  | 0.750         | 0.237      | 0.70                  | 0.798         | 0.203      |

**Table 5.3: Precision-Constraint Sweep — Transactional Feature Set (seed=42, validation-set metrics)**

| Precision Constraint | Trans RF Threshold | Val Precision | Val Recall | Trans GB Threshold | Val Precision | Val Recall |
| :------------------- | :----------------- | :------------ | :--------- | :----------------- | :------------ | :--------- |
| 0.40                 | 0.60               | 0.464         | 0.346      | 0.35               | 0.471         | 0.434      |
| 0.50                 | 0.65               | 0.511         | 0.244      | 0.45               | 0.520         | 0.224      |
| 0.60                 | 0.75               | 0.598         | 0.118      | 0.70               | 0.400         | 0.009      |
| 0.70                 | 0.80               | 0.636         | 0.045      | 0.70               | 0.400         | 0.009      |
| 0.80                 | 0.85               | —             | —          | 0.50               | 0.503         | 0.175      |

**Note:** Transactional Logistic Regression could not meet any precision constraint ≤ 0.80 at any threshold and is omitted from the sweep table.

**Constraint Selection Rationale:**

The sweep reveals a steep precision-recall tradeoff:

1. **Below 0.50**, recall is high (60–79%) but precision drops below 50%, meaning more than half of flagged transactions are false positives.
2. **At 0.50**, Combined GB achieves 57.5% recall at 53.2% precision, and Combined RF achieves 60.0% recall at 48.9% precision — representing the best F1 balance across both models.
3. **Above 0.60**, recall drops sharply (to \~43%) with diminishing precision gains, reducing operational utility.
4. **At 0.90** (initial constraint), most models cannot find viable thresholds, as demonstrated in Section 5.3.1.

A precision constraint of **0.50** was selected as the optimal operating point based on:

- **Peak F1 balance** across both Combined RF and GB models.
- **Meaningful recall** (\~57–60%), enabling detection of more than half of fraudulent transactions.
- **Alignment with literature**: Prior work has shown that default thresholds, usually \~0.5, are often suboptimal in fraud detection tasks, and that performance can be significantly improved through metric-driven threshold optimization.

![][image9]

#### 5.3.4 Key Observations {#5.3.4-key-observations}

**Behavioral models excel at bot detection.** All three learning algorithms achieve ROC-AUC \> 0.91 and PR-AUC \> 0.84 for the behavioral (is_bot) classification task. Gradient Boosting achieves the highest F1 (0.730 ± 0.022), followed by Random Forest (0.685 ± 0.052) and Logistic Regression (0.662 ± 0.021). The small standard deviations confirm consistent performance across seeds. All models substantially exceed the DummyClassifier baseline (PR-AUC 0.171), confirming that the behavioral features capture genuine signals. Note that ROC-AUC and PR-AUC are threshold-independent and thus identical to the 0.90 constraint results; only precision, recall, and F1 change due to the lower threshold selection.

**Transactional-only models show limited discrimination.** The transactional feature set alone achieves modest ROC-AUC (0.711–0.717 for RF/GB) but low PR-AUC (0.419–0.426). Even under the relaxed precision ≥ 0.50 constraint, transactional models exhibit high variance in recall: RF achieves 0.341 ± 0.280 while GB achieves only 0.134 ± 0.104. Logistic Regression is particularly poor (ROC-AUC 0.563, recall 0.021), indicating that the relationship between transactional features and fraud is substantially non-linear.

**Combined models significantly improve fraud detection.** Adding behavioral features to the transactional set increases PR-AUC from 0.426 to 0.584 for Gradient Boosting (+37%) and from 0.419 to 0.583 for Random Forest (+39%). Critically, recall improves dramatically: Combined GB achieves 0.524 ± 0.065 vs. Transactional GB's 0.134 ± 0.104 — a nearly 4× improvement, while RF exhibits a \~50% improvement. Combined RF and GB achieve F1 scores above 0.51, representing operationally meaningful fraud detection.

**Ensemble methods outperform Logistic Regression for fraud detection.** For the combined feature set, GB achieves PR-AUC 0.584 vs. LR's 0.322 — an 81% improvement. LR's recall collapses to 0.020 ± 0.020 even under the relaxed constraint, confirming that non-linear modeling is essential for capturing the complex interactions between behavioral and transactional features.

**GB vs. RF: marginal differences.** Gradient Boosting and Random Forest achieve nearly identical PR-AUC for the combined set (0.584 vs. 0.583). GB shows slightly higher and more stable recall (0.524 ± 0.065 vs. 0.495 ± 0.097), while RF achieves slightly higher precision (0.548 ± 0.055 vs. 0.529 ± 0.026). The differences are within one standard deviation, suggesting that the choice between these ensemble methods is less critical than the choice of feature set.

### 5.4 Threshold Sensitivity Analysis {#5.4-threshold-sensitivity-analysis}

A key operational parameter in fraud detection systems is the choice of decision threshold. This section introduces a three-tier policy, defined using two thresholds, T_low and T_high, alongside with an operational threshold, T_op, which serves as a reference threshold derived from the precision-recall sweep. This operational point provides a range for T_low to vary, according to operational needs, taking into account that a stricter T_low will reduce fraud at the cost of increasing operational load, by increasing the amount of cases routed to REVIEW. For our current scope, a conservative T_low was retained to prioritize a robust fraud detection performance. Additionally, in this section we are examining threshold sensitivity across models, plotting our main metrics (Recall, precision and F1) against the selected threshold range.

#### 5.4.1 Three-Threshold Decision Framework {#5.4.1-three-threshold-decision-framework}

The pipeline produces a fraud probability score per transaction and maps it to an operational decision using three thresholds, each tuned on the validation set (20% of training data, separate from test set) to avoid optimistic bias:

- **T_high (DENY threshold, precision ≥ 0.70)**: The lowest threshold where at least 70% of flagged transactions are truly fraudulent. Transactions scoring ≥ T_high are automatically denied. The 70% precision requirement ensures that auto-blocking carries high confidence — even with relatively low recall, the cost of incorrectly blocking a legitimate transaction is offset by the certainty that most denied transactions are genuine fraud.  

- **T_op (Operational threshold, precision ≥ 0.50)**: The highest-recall threshold that still maintains at least 50% precision. This represents the recommended operating point that balances detection coverage with false positive control. T_op is reported as an industry-oriented suggestion: the optimal operating point will depend on the implementing company's capacity to maintain fraud review teams and its willingness to accept higher fraud probabilities in order to optimize operations. For this thesis, T_op serves as a reference point for model comparison and hypothesis testing.  

- **T_low (REVIEW threshold, recall ≥ 0.95)**: The highest threshold that captures at least 95% of known fraud. Transactions scoring between T_low and T_high are flagged for manual review, ensuring that most fraudulent cases are captured within the review or denial tiers. Consistently with prior work on cost-sensitive decision-making, a conservative T_low is adopted to prioritize recall, reflecting the higher cost of false negatives in relation to manual review costs (Elkan, 2001; Hancock et al., 2022). This makes it economically rational to accept a larger review queue to minimize fraud leakage.

The resulting decision zones are:

| Score Range             | Decision   | Action                                |
| :---------------------- | :--------- | :------------------------------------ |
| Score ≥ T_high          | **DENY**   | Auto-block (precision ≥ 70%)          |
| T_low ≤ Score \< T_high | **REVIEW** | Manual review queue (recall \< 95%)   |
| Score \< T_low          | **ALLOW**  | Auto-approve (low risk, recall ≥ 95%) |

Table 5.5 presents the three thresholds selected for each model configuration at seed=42.

**Table 5.5: Three-Threshold Decision System (seed=42)**

| Feature Set   | Algorithm | T_high (DENY) | T_op (Operational) | T_low (REVIEW) | % Allow | % Review | % Deny |
| :------------ | :-------- | :------------ | :----------------- | :------------- | :------ | :------- | :----- |
| Behavioral    | LR        | 0.45          | 0.35               | 0.05           | 5.3%    | 73.3%    | 21.5%  |
| Behavioral    | RF        | 0.25          | 0.15               | 0.05           | 1.3%    | 76.8%    | 21.9%  |
| Behavioral    | GB        | 0.10          | 0.10               | 0.05           | 66.4%   | 11.6%    | 22.1%  |
| Transactional | LR        | 0.75          | 0.60               | 0.50           | 2.1%    | 97.6%    | 0.3%   |
| Transactional | RF        | 0.80          | 0.65               | 0.30           | 26.0%   | 73.3%    | 0.8%   |
| Transactional | GB        | 0.60          | 0.45               | 0.10           | 19.1%   | 79.4%    | 1.5%   |
| Combined      | LR        | 0.75          | 0.60               | 0.50           | 3.2%    | 96.6%    | 0.3%   |
| Combined      | RF        | 0.65          | 0.50               | 0.25           | 23.5%   | 64.2%    | 12.3%  |
| Combined      | GB        | 0.50          | 0.30               | 0.10           | 21.1%   | 65.7%    | 13.2%  |

**Generated figures:**

### 5.5 Stealth Bot Detection {#5.5-stealth-bot-detection}

A key research question (RQ1) concerns the effectiveness of behavioral features in detecting bot-driven transactions, with particular attention to stealth bots that deliberately mimic human behavior. This section examines stealth bot recall across models and seeds.

#### 5.5.1 Stealth Bot Recall Across Seeds {#5.5.1-stealth-bot-recall-across-seeds}

Table 5.6 presents the stealth bot recall (i.e., the proportion of stealth bot transactions correctly flagged as positive) at the best validation-selected threshold for each behavioral model across all five seeds.

**Table 5.6: Stealth Bot Recall at Best Threshold (Behavioral Models, precision ≥ 0.50)**

| Algorithm | Seed 42 | Seed 123 | Seed 456 | Seed 789 | Seed 2024 | Mean ± Std    |
| :-------- | :------ | :------- | :------- | :------- | :-------- | :------------ |
| LR        | 0.955   | 0.949    | 0.908    | 0.929    | 0.990     | 0.946 ± 0.030 |
| RF        | 0.977   | 0.939    | 0.882    | 0.949    | 0.990     | 0.947 ± 0.042 |
| GB        | 0.898   | 0.908    | 0.868    | 0.918    | 0.947     | 0.908 ± 0.029 |

**Table 5.7: Overall Bot Recall at Best Threshold (Behavioral Models, for comparison)**

| Algorithm | Mean Recall (all bots) | Mean Stealth Recall | Recall Gap |
| :-------- | :--------------------- | :------------------ | :--------- |
| LR        | 0.861 ± 0.021          | 0.946 ± 0.030       | \+0.085    |
| RF        | 0.863 ± 0.025          | 0.947 ± 0.042       | \+0.084    |
| GB        | 0.845 ± 0.026          | 0.908 ± 0.029       | \+0.063    |

#### 5.5.2 Analysis {#5.5.2-analysis}

**Stealth bot recall exceeds overall bot recall** Under the precision ≥ 0.50 constraint, all three algorithms achieve stealth recall above 0.90, with positive recall gaps ranging from \+0.063 (GB) to \+0.085 (LR). This occurs because the relatively low threshold captures nearly all stealth bots. In contrast, the overall bot recall is affected by the label noise in the `is_bot` variable, where some human profiles were intentionally mislabeled as bots. These instances exhibit genuine human behavioral patterns, and the models correctly classified them as humans. However, due to their incorrect labels, they are counted as false-negatives, artificially lowering the overall recall. The higher stealth recall suggests that the model is effectively capturing subtle behavioral signals, even when some noise labels were introduced in order to imitate real world datasets limitations.

**Random Forest and Logistic Regression achieve near-perfect stealth detection.** Both RF and LR achieve mean stealth recall above 0.94, indicating that at lower thresholds, the behavioral features provide sufficient separation to detect nearly all stealth bots. GB achieves 0.908 ± 0.029, slightly lower but still excellent.

**The threshold-stealth tradeoff is key.** Comparing these results with the initial 0.90 constraint (where stealth recall ranged from 0.626–0.793), the relaxed precision constraint dramatically improves stealth detection — stealth recall increases by 19–51% depending on the algorithm. This illustrates that the precision-constraint choice directly controls the ability to detect sophisticated bot behavior: strict constraints sacrifice stealth detection for fewer false positives.

**Seed variability remains moderate.** Standard deviations for stealth recall (0.029–0.042) are comparable to overall recall variability (0.021–0.026), suggesting more stable stealth detection under the relaxed constraint than under the strict 0.90 constraint (where stealth std was 0.061–0.066).

#### 5.5.3 Stealth Bot Detection in Combined Models {#5.5.3-stealth-bot-detection-in-combined-models}

For the combined (is_fraud) models, stealth bot recall can be inferred from the fraud detection perspective. The combined feature set inherits the behavioral signal that enables stealth detection, while adding transactional risk indicators. At seed=42:

| Feature Set | Algorithm | Stealth Recall | Flag Rate |
| :---------- | :-------- | :------------- | :-------- |
| Behavioral  | RF        | 0.977          | 31.9%     |
| Combined    | RF        | 0.719          | 33.5%     |
| Behavioral  | GB        | 0.898          | 22.1%     |
| Combined    | GB        | 0.754          | 33.1%     |

Under the optimized precision ≥ 0.50 constraint, the combined models flag a meaningful fraction of transactions (33%), enabling stealth-level analysis. Combined models achieve lower stealth recall than behavioral models (0.719–0.754 vs. 0.898–0.977) because the combined target (is_fraud) is a stricter criterion than is_bot — not all bot transactions are fraudulent. The combined models detect stealth bots only when their transactions also exhibit fraud risk factors (high-risk transaction types, large amounts, drain patterns), while the behavioral models detect stealth bots based solely on behavioral deviation.

### 5.6 Feature Importance Analysis {#5.6-feature-importance-analysis}

Understanding which features drive model predictions is critical for both interpretability and regulatory compliance. This section examines feature importance rankings from the tree-based models (Random Forest and Gradient Boosting) and absolute coefficient magnitudes from Logistic Regression. Feature importances are reported from the seed=42 experiment.

#### 5.6.1 Behavioral Feature Importance {#5.6.1-behavioral-feature-importance}

For the behavioral (is_bot) task, feature importance rankings are remarkably consistent across all three algorithms:

**Table 5.8: Top Behavioral Features by Model (seed=42)**

| Rank | Random Forest            | Gradient Boosting        | Logistic Regression      |
| :--- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| 1    | keystroke_flight_time_ms | keystroke_flight_time_ms | keystroke_flight_time_ms |
| 2    | session_duration_sec     | session_duration_sec     | session_duration_sec     |
| 3    | checkout_velocity_sec    | checkout_velocity_sec    | checkout_velocity_sec    |
| 4    | mouse_speed_variance     | mouse_speed_variance     | mouse_speed_variance     |
| 5    | pages_visited            | pages_visited            | is_datacenter_ip         |
| 6    | is_datacenter_ip         | is_datacenter_ip         | pages_visited            |
| 7    | impossible_travel_flag   | impossible_travel_flag   | impossible_travel_flag   |

**Key observations:**

- **Keystroke flight time** is the single most important feature across all models, consistent with the behavioral biometrics literature (Ahmed & Traore, 2007; Acien et al., 2022). The large distributional gap between humans (150 ± 50 ms) and standard bots (50 ± 5 ms) provides strong discriminative signals.
- **Session duration and checkout velocity** rank second and third, reflecting the fundamental speed difference between automated scripts and human browsing behavior.
- **Mouse speed variance** captures the lower behavioral variability of bots, particularly stealth bots.
- **Network indicators** (is_datacenter_ip, impossible_travel_flag) rank lower despite being strong individual signals, likely because they are binary features with limited information content compared to continuous behavioral features.

#### 5.6.2 Transactional Feature Importance {#5.6.2-transactional-feature-importance}

For the transactional (is_fraud) task, feature importance rankings differ more across algorithms:

**Table 5.9: Top Transactional Features by Model (seed=42)**

| Rank | Random Forest       | Gradient Boosting      | Logistic Regression    |
| :--- | :------------------ | :--------------------- | :--------------------- |
| 1    | amount              | balance_drain_ratio    | balance_drain_ratio    |
| 2    | balance_drain_ratio | is_full_drain          | is_full_drain          |
| 3    | old_balance_origin  | amount                 | amount                 |
| 4    | new_balance_origin  | old_balance_origin     | dest_balance_unchanged |
| 5    | is_full_drain       | dest_balance_unchanged | old_balance_origin     |

**Key observations:**

- **Derived features are highly valuable.** The `balance_drain_ratio` and `is_full_drain` features consistently rank among the top features, validating the feature engineering decision to compute these from raw balance fields. In PaySim, 97.6% of fraudulent TRANSFER transactions drain the origin account to zero (Lopez-Rojas et al., 2016), making `is_full_drain` a powerful fraud indicator.
- **Amount** is important across all models, reflecting the literature finding that fraudulent transactions tend to involve larger amounts.
- **Transaction type** (one-hot encoded) provides moderate signal, as fraud concentrates in TRANSFER and CASH_OUT types.

#### 5.6.3 Combined Feature Importance {#5.6.3-combined-feature-importance}

The combined model uses all 16 features (7 behavioral \+ 9 transactional). Feature importance rankings reveal how the model balances information from both signal types:

**Table 5.10: Top Combined Features by Model (seed=42)**

| Rank | Random Forest            | Gradient Boosting        | Logistic Regression      |
| :--- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| 1    | keystroke_flight_time_ms | keystroke_flight_time_ms | keystroke_flight_time_ms |
| 2    | session_duration_sec     | balance_drain_ratio      | session_duration_sec     |
| 3    | amount                   | session_duration_sec     | balance_drain_ratio      |
| 4    | balance_drain_ratio      | amount                   | checkout_velocity_sec    |
| 5    | checkout_velocity_sec    | is_full_drain            | is_full_drain            |
| 6    | mouse_speed_variance     | checkout_velocity_sec    | mouse_speed_variance     |
| 7    | old_balance_origin       | mouse_speed_variance     | amount                   |

**Key observations:**

- **Behavioral features dominate the top ranks** even for the fraud detection task. Keystroke flight time remains the \#1 feature in the combined model, reflecting the strong correlation between bot behavior and fraud (bots are enriched in the fraudulent population by design).
- **Transactional derived features complement behavioral signals.** Features like `balance_drain_ratio` and `is_full_drain` rank among the top 5, contributing fraud-specific information that behavioral features alone cannot capture.
- **Cross-domain features provide complementary signals.** The combined model leverages both behavioral timing features (keystroke, session duration) and transactional risk indicators (drain ratio, amount), confirming that the two feature types carry non-redundant information.

#### 5.6.4 Feature Redundancy Analysis {#5.6.4-feature-redundancy-analysis}

The feature correlation matrix (Section 5.2.7) provides insight into redundancy. Expected high-correlation pairs include:

- **old_balance_origin ↔ new_balance_origin**: Mechanically linked (new \= old − amount).
- **amount ↔ balance_drain_ratio**: Ratio partially derived from amount.
- **is_datacenter_ip ↔ is_bot**: Datacenter IP is a strong bot indicator by design.

Low correlation between behavioral and transactional feature groups confirms that feature fusion provides genuine value rather than redundant signal. This validates the combined model's improvement over individual feature sets (Section 5.3).

### 5.7 Ablation Study {#5.7-ablation-study}

While a full ablation study with re-training is beyond the scope of the current experimental runs, the feature importance analysis (Section 5.6) and the three-feature-set comparison (behavioral, transactional, combined) provide a natural ablation framework. This section synthesizes ablation insights from the existing experimental design.

#### 5.7.1 Feature Set Ablation (Behavioral vs. Transactional vs. Combined) {#5.7.1-feature-set-ablation-(behavioral-vs.-transactional-vs.-combined)}

The primary ablation compares three feature sets to isolate the contribution of each signal type:

**Table 5.11: Feature Set Ablation — PR-AUC (mean ± std, 5 seeds)**

| Algorithm | Behavioral (is_bot) | Transactional (is_fraud) | Combined (is_fraud) | Δ Combined vs. Trans |
| :-------- | :------------------ | :----------------------- | :------------------ | :------------------- |
| LR        | 0.841 ± 0.023       | 0.322 ± 0.010            | 0.322 ± 0.011       | \+0.0%               |
| RF        | 0.852 ± 0.026       | 0.419 ± 0.016            | 0.583 ± 0.013       | \+39.1%              |
| GB        | 0.852 ± 0.024       | 0.426 ± 0.020            | 0.584 ± 0.012       | \+37.1%              |

**Key findings:**

- **Adding behavioral features to transactional features produces a \~38% improvement** in PR-AUC for ensemble methods (RF, GB), confirming the value of multi-modal feature fusion (H3).
- **Logistic Regression does not benefit from feature fusion** (PR-AUC 0.322 for both transactional and combined), indicating that the linear model cannot exploit the cross-domain interactions between behavioral and transactional features. The added dimensionality introduces no gain without non-linear capacity.
- **Behavioral features alone** outperform transactional and combined features for bot detection (PR-AUC \~0.85 vs. \~0.58), reflecting the different target variables and the stronger signal-to-noise ratio in behavioral bot detection.

#### 5.7.2 Impact of Derived Features {#5.7.2-impact-of-derived-features}

The derived features (`balance_drain_ratio`, `is_full_drain`, `dest_balance_unchanged`) are engineered from raw balance fields and consistently rank among the top transactional features (Section 5.6.2). Their importance can be assessed indirectly:

- **balance_drain_ratio** and **is_full_drain** together capture the account-draining signal that is the hallmark of PaySim fraud (97.6% of fraudulent TRANSFERs drain the origin to zero). Without these features, models would need to learn the drain pattern from raw balance differences, which is a more complex task.
- **dest_balance_unchanged** captures the anomaly where the destination account balance does not increase after receiving funds — a pattern present in \~50.9% of PaySim fraud.

The high feature importance of these derived features across all model types suggests that removing them would degrade transactional and combined model performance. A formal ablation (training without these features) is recommended as future work to quantify the exact impact.

#### 5.7.3 Impact of Network Indicators {#5.7.3-impact-of-network-indicators}

The binary network features (`is_datacenter_ip`, `impossible_travel_flag`) rank lower in tree-based feature importance (Section 5.6.1, ranks 6–7), but this does not mean they are irrelevant:

- **is_datacenter_ip** has a strong bot detection signal (70% of standard bots vs. 0% of normal humans), but as a binary feature it provides limited splitting power compared to continuous features.
- **impossible_travel_flag** is relatively rare even among bots (60% standard, 25% stealth, 2% human), making it a high-precision but low-recall indicator.

These features likely contribute most at specific decision boundaries where other features are ambiguous, making their removal potentially harmful for edge cases (e.g., stealth bots that pass other behavioral checks).

#### 5.7.4 Balance Capping Effect {#5.7.4-balance-capping-effect}

The `generate_transaction_features` function caps transaction amounts to a random fraction (5–85%) of available balance to prevent unrealistic overspend. This capping:

- **Reduces accidental full drains** in the legitimate population, making `is_full_drain` a more reliable fraud indicator.
- **Creates a more realistic amount distribution** that better matches PaySim's observed patterns.
- **May compress the amount range**, potentially reducing the discriminative power of the amount feature for models that rely heavily on amount thresholds.

A controlled comparison with and without balance capping would quantify this tradeoff and is suggested as future work.

### 5.8 Discussion {#5.8-discussion}

This section synthesizes the experimental findings by explicitly addressing each research question and hypothesis, comparing results against literature benchmarks, and discussing limitations and unexpected findings.

#### 5.8.1 Answering the Research Questions {#5.8.1-answering-the-research-questions}

**RQ1 — Behavioral detection effectiveness:** _How effectively can behavioral features distinguish bot-driven from human-driven transactions?_

Behavioral features achieve strong bot detection performance, with ROC-AUC of 0.919 ± 0.015 (RF) and PR-AUC of 0.852 ± 0.026 (RF) across five seeds. Keystroke flight time, session duration, and checkout velocity are the top discriminative features. Under the optimized precision ≥ 0.50 constraint, stealth bot recall reaches 0.947 ± 0.042 (RF), exceeding overall bot recall (0.863 ± 0.025). The positive recall gap (+0.084) is likely explained by the impact of noisy `is_bot` labels on overall recall rather than a genuine stealth advantage, but confirms that the relaxed constraint enables near-complete stealth detection.

**RQ2 — Transactional detection effectiveness:** _How effectively can transactional features identify fraudulent transactions when calibrated against PaySim?_

Transactional features alone provide moderate discrimination (ROC-AUC 0.711–0.717 for ensembles) but limited operational utility. Even under the relaxed precision ≥ 0.50 constraint, transactional models achieve PR-AUC of only 0.419–0.426, with high recall variance (RF: 0.341 ± 0.280). Under the initial 0.90 constraint (Section 5.3.1), most transactional models could not find viable operating thresholds at all. The derived features (balance_drain_ratio, is_full_drain) are critical — without them, transactional detection would be even weaker. This result aligns with the literature finding that transactional features alone are insufficient for robust fraud detection in highly imbalanced settings (Carcillo et al., 2018).

**RQ3 — Combined model value:** _Does combining behavioral and transactional features improve fraud detection quality?_

Yes, decisively for ensemble methods. The combined feature set improves PR-AUC by approximately 38% over transactional-only for both RF (0.583 vs. 0.419) and GB (0.584 vs. 0.426). ROC-AUC improves from \~0.71 to \~0.78. Recall improves nearly 4× for GB (0.524 vs. 0.134) and nearly 50% for RF (0,495 vs. 0.341). However, Logistic Regression shows no improvement from feature fusion (PR-AUC 0.322 for both), indicating that non-linear modeling capacity is necessary to exploit cross-domain feature interactions.

**RQ4 — Model comparison and threshold sensitivity:** _How do different algorithms compare, and how sensitive are operational decisions to threshold selection?_

Gradient Boosting and Random Forest perform comparably across all feature sets, with GB showing a marginal edge in PR-AUC for the combined fraud task (0.584 vs. 0.583). Both substantially outperform Logistic Regression for fraud detection. Operational decisions are highly sensitive to the precision constraint: the precision-constraint sweep (Section 5.3.2) shows that relaxing from 0.90 to 0.50 increases Combined GB recall from near-zero to 52.4% ± 6.5% across five seeds, a dramatic operational improvement that motivated the constraint selection.

#### 5.8.2 Hypothesis Evaluation {#5.8.2-hypothesis-evaluation}

**Table 5.12: Hypothesis Evaluation Summary**

| Hypothesis                                                                          | Verdict                 | Evidence                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| H1: Behavioral features achieve strong bot detection, stealth bots harder to detect | **Supported**           | RF PR-AUC 0.852 ± 0.026, ROC-AUC 0.919 ± 0.015. Under precision ≥ 0.50, stealth recall (0.947) exceeds overall recall (0.863) due to label noise effects; under 0.90 constraint, stealth gap confirmed (0.006–0.126). |
| H2: Transactional features achieve ROC-AUC \> 0.70                                  | **Supported**           | Transactional RF achieves ROC-AUC 0.711 ± 0.010 and GB achieves 0.717 ± 0.009, both exceeding the 0.70 threshold.                                                                                                     |
| H3: Combined model outperforms transactional models in ROC-AUC and recall           | **Supported**           | Combined outperforms transactional-only in ROC-AUC: RF 0.781 vs. 0.711 (+10%), GB 0.780 vs. 0.717 (+9%). PR-AUC improves \~38%. Recall improves \~4× for GB and \~50%. LR shows no combined improvement.              |
| H4: Random Forest outperforms GB and LR on ROC-AUC for fraud detection              | **Partially supported** | Combined RF (0.781) and GB (0.780) are nearly identical in ROC-AUC (within one std dev). Both massively outperform LR (0.566). RF leads for behavioral bot detection (0.919 vs. 0.918).                               |

#### 5.8.3 Comparison Against Literature Benchmarks {#5.8.3-comparison-against-literature-benchmarks}

**Table 5.13: Literature Comparison**

| Study                         | Dataset                       | Method           | ROC-AUC   | PR-AUC    | Notes                          |
| :---------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :--------------- | :-------- | :-------- | :----------------------------- |
| Lopez-Rojas et al. (2016)     | PaySim (6.3M txn)             | RF               | 0.97      | —         | Original PaySim, full features |
| Carcillo et al. (2018)        | Real bank data                | RF (streaming)   | —         | 0.52      | Real-time with concept drift   |
| Alarfaj et al. (2022)         | Credit card                   | XGBoost          | 0.98      | —         | PCA-transformed features       |
| Hilal et al. (2022)           | PaySim \+ others              | LightGBM         | 0.99      | —         | Best-case survey results       |
| Kaggle community (2020–2023)  | PaySim (Kaggle)               | XGBoost/LightGBM | 0.97–0.99 | 0.85–0.95 | Risk of leakage                |
| **This work (Combined GB)**   | Synthetic (PaySim-calibrated) | GB               | **0.78**  | **0.58**  | **Strict val/test separation** |
| **This work (Combined RF)**   | Synthetic (PaySim-calibrated) | RF               | **0.78**  | **0.58**  | **Strict val/test separation** |
| **This work (Behavioral RF)** | Synthetic (PaySim-calibrated) | RF               | **0.92**  | **0.85**  | **Bot detection task**         |

**Interpretation:**

Our results are lower than reported in many PaySim studies (ROC-AUC 0.80 vs. 0.97–0.99). This gap is expected and attributable to several factors:

1. **Synthetic data limitations.** Our dataset is generated from PaySim-calibrated distributions rather than the original PaySim data, introducing distributional mismatches and potentially weaker fraud signals.
2. **Strict evaluation protocol.** Our threshold is selected on a validation set and evaluated on a held-out test set, avoiding the optimistic bias present in studies that tune on the test set. Kaggle leaderboard solutions, for instance, often exhibit threshold snooping.
3. **Feature set differences.** Original PaySim studies use the full 6.3M-transaction dataset with direct access to ground-truth labels, while our synthetic dataset has 10,000 samples with probabilistically assigned fraud labels.
4. **Deliberate noise.** We inject 3% label noise and 5% measurement noise to simulate real-world conditions, which degrades all model performance.

The PR-AUC of 0.52 reported by Carcillo et al. (2018) on real bank data is the most appropriate benchmark, as it uses a similar evaluation framework. Our Combined models achieve PR-AUC of 0.584 ± 0.012 (GB) and 0.583 ± 0.013 (RF), both slightly exceeding this benchmark, suggesting that the synthetic pipeline produces results in a realistic performance range.

#### 5.8.4 Limitations {#5.8.4-limitations}

1. **Synthetic data fidelity.** Despite PaySim calibration, the synthetic data may not capture all fraud patterns present in real-world data (e.g., temporal patterns, network effects, multi-step fraud schemes). The generalizability of results to real-world deployment is uncertain.  

2. **Dataset size.** With 10,000 samples per seed, the dataset is small compared to production fraud detection systems that process millions of transactions. Larger datasets would provide more stable estimates and potentially enable more complex models.  

3. **Fraud label assignment.** Fraud labels are assigned probabilistically based on risk factors, rather than through a deterministic ground-truth process. This introduces label uncertainty that affects model training and evaluation.  

4. **Feature simplification.** Real-world fraud detection systems use hundreds of features including device fingerprinting, IP reputation scores, historical account behavior, and social network analysis. Our 16-feature set is a simplified representation.  

5. **Single-stage evaluation.** The three-tier decision system (ALLOW/REVIEW/DENY) is evaluated in a static, batch setting. Production systems operate in real-time with concept drift, feedback loops, and adversarial adaptation.  

6. **Precision constraint selection.** The precision constraint was reduced from 0.90 to 0.50 based on a systematic sweep (Section 5.3.2) and alignment with prior findings that threshold optimized under operational specific objectives outperform default or heuristic thresholds. A formal cost-benefit analysis incorporating false-positive and false-negative costs would yield more operationally relevant thresholds (see Section 6.4).

#### 5.8.5 Unexpected Findings {#5.8.5-unexpected-findings}

1. **Logistic Regression's failure to benefit from feature fusion** was unexpected. While LR is a simpler model, the expectation was that additional features would provide at least marginal improvement. Instead, the combined LR model's PR-AUC (0.322) is identical to the transactional-only LR (0.322), suggesting that the behavioral features introduce collinearity or noise that the linear model cannot separate from the fraud signal.  

2. **The severity of the 0.90 precision constraint** was more extreme than anticipated. Many model configurations could not find any threshold satisfying this constraint on the validation set, particularly for transactional-only models (Section 5.3.1). This motivated the precision-constraint sweep (Section 5.3.2) and the subsequent adoption of the 0.50 constraint, which enabled operationally meaningful recall across most configurations.  

3. **Stealth bot recall exceeding overall bot recall under the relaxed constraint** was counterintuitive, although understandable. Under precision ≥ 0.50, stealth recall (0.908–0.947) exceeded overall bot recall (0.845–0.863) across all algorithms. This reversal is attributable to noisy is_bot labels assigned to human profiles — the model correctly classifies these as non-bot, reducing aggregate recall while actual stealth bots (with genuine bot-like behavioral patterns) are reliably detected at lower thresholds.

---

## 6\. Conclusion {#6.-conclusion}

### 6.1 Summary of Contributions {#6.1-summary-of-contributions}

This dissertation has presented a complete end-to-end framework for fraud detection in FinTech platforms that integrates behavioral and transactional features within a supervised machine learning pipeline. The main contributions are:

1. **Hybrid synthetic data generation.** A pipeline that combines PaySim-calibrated transactional distributions with literature-grounded behavioral features across four user profiles (human_normal, human_fast, bot_standard, bot_stealth). This approach enables reproducible experimentation on multi-modal fraud detection without requiring access to proprietary financial data, which was one of the major limitations when starting this project.  

2. **Quantified value of feature fusion.** A systematic evaluation demonstrating that adding behavioral features to transactional models improves ROC-AUC by \~10% (from 0.71 to 0.78) and PR-AUC by \~38% (from 0.42 to 0.58) for ensemble methods. This confirms that behavioral and transactional features carry complementary, non-redundant fraud signals.  

3. **Rigorous threshold tuning methodology.** A three-tier decision system (ALLOW/REVIEW/DENY) with thresholds selected exclusively on a validation set under a precision constraint, preventing the test-set snooping common in published fraud detection studies. The threshold policy, alongside the operational point suggestion, represents not only a good academic contribution, but a clear alignment with real-world deployment practices.  

4. **Multi-seed statistical validation.** All experiments repeated across five independent seeds with mean ± standard deviation reporting, ensuring that findings are not artifacts of a single data partition.  

5. **Precision-constraint sweep.** A systematic analysis varying the precision constraint from 0.40 to 0.80, quantifying the steep precision-recall tradeoff and providing operational guidance for threshold selection at different risk tolerance levels.

### 6.2 Key Findings {#6.2-key-findings}

The experimental evaluation across 12 configurations (4 algorithms × 3 feature sets) repeated over 5 seeds yielded the following principal findings:

**F1 — Behavioral features are highly effective for bot detection.** All three learning algorithms achieved ROC-AUC \> 0.91 and PR-AUC \> 0.84 on the behavioral (is_bot) task. Random Forest achieved the best overall performance (ROC-AUC 0.919 ± 0.015, PR-AUC 0.852 ± 0.026). Gradient Boosting achieved the highest F1 (0.730 ± 0.022) under the optimized precision ≥ 0.50 constraint. Keystroke flight time, session duration, and checkout velocity were consistently the top three discriminative features.

**F2 — Stealth bots are reliably detectable under the relaxed constraint.** Under precision ≥ 0.50, stealth bot recall reached 0.946–0.947 for LR and RF, exceeding overall bot recall (0.861–0.863). This apparent reversal is primarily driven by label noise in the `is_bot` variable, which depresses aggregate recall rather than reflecting a true advantage in detecting stealth behavior. Under the initial 0.90 constraint, stealth recall was lower (0.626–0.793), confirming that strict precision requirements disproportionately impact stealth detection.

**F3 — Feature fusion substantially improves fraud detection for ensemble methods.** Adding behavioral features to transactional models improved PR-AUC by \~38% for both RF and GB, and recall by nearly 4× for GB (0.524 vs. 0.134). This confirms that behavioral and transactional signals carry complementary information. Logistic Regression, however, showed no benefit from fusion (PR-AUC 0.322 for both), indicating that non-linear modeling capacity is essential to exploit cross-domain interactions.

**F4 — The 0.90 precision constraint is operationally severe.** Under this constraint, most transactional-only models could not find viable thresholds, and even combined models achieved limited recall (4–17% at seed=42). The precision-constraint sweep identified 0.50 as the optimal operating point, under which Combined GB achieves a recall of 52.4% ± 6.5% across five seeds, highlighting the critical importance of threshold selection for practical deployment.

**F5 — Random Forest and Gradient Boosting perform comparably.** GB slightly edged RF in PR-AUC for the combined fraud task (0.584 vs. 0.583), but the difference fell within one standard deviation. RF achieved slightly higher precision (0.548 vs. 0.529) while GB showed more stable recall (0.524 ± 0.065 vs. 0.495 ± 0.097). The choice between these algorithms is less impactful than the choice of feature set.

**F6 — Results are statistically robust.** Standard deviations across five seeds were small for ranking metrics (ROC-AUC σ ≤ 0.016, PR-AUC σ ≤ 0.026), confirming that model rankings are stable across data partitions. Operational metrics (precision, recall at threshold) showed higher variability, particularly for transactional models (RF recall std 0.280), reflecting the difficulty of fraud detection with transactional features alone.

### 6.3 Limitations {#6.3-limitations}

Several limitations should be considered when interpreting these results:

1. **Synthetic data fidelity.** Although calibrated from PaySim, the synthetic data does not capture all real-world complexity — temporal patterns, multi-step fraud schemes, network effects between accounts, and concept drift are absent. The generalizability of results to production environments remains to be validated.  

2. **Probabilistic fraud labels.** Fraud labels are assigned through an additive probability model rather than through a deterministic ground-truth process. This introduces label uncertainty that may suppress model performance relative to what could be achieved with clean labels, and it prevents direct comparison with studies using ground-truth-labeled datasets.  

3. **Limited algorithm scope.** The evaluation covers four algorithms (Dummy, LR, RF, GB). Deep learning approaches (autoencoders, LSTMs, transformer-based models), which may capture more complex temporal and sequential patterns, were not explored.  

4. **Dataset scale.** With 10,000 samples per seed, the dataset is small by production standards. Larger datasets would provide more stable threshold selection and potentially enable more complex models.  

5. **No real-world deployment.** The three-tier decision system is evaluated in a static, batch setting. Real-world deployment introduces additional challenges including latency requirements, feedback loops, adversarial adaptation, and model monitoring for performance degradation.  

6. **PaySim as reference.** PaySim is itself a simulation of mobile money transactions, not real banking data. While it is the most widely used benchmark in fraud detection research, it may not fully represent the diversity and complexity of real-world FinTech fraud patterns.  

7. **Real-world data privacy protection.** One of the biggest limitations for using real-world data in this project is the compliance with GDPR as well as the possibility to access company production level datasets. However the synthetical data generated was validated to match real world cases, this may fall short to fully represent fraud within the selected features.

### 6.4 Future Work {#6.4-future-work}

Several directions for future research emerge from this work:

**Real-time scoring pipeline.** Extending the batch evaluation to a real-time scoring architecture using event-driven processing (e.g., Apache Kafka, AWS Kinesis) would enable evaluation of latency constraints and throughput requirements for production deployment.

**Expanded feature space.** Incorporating additional signal types — device fingerprinting, IP reputation scores, historical account velocity features, and social network indicators — could improve discrimination, particularly for the transactional fraud task where current features show limited separability.

**Expanded datasets sampling.** Increasing datasets samples would allow further analysis of model performance. While on this project we used a relatively small sample amount (10000) to optimize time and resources, having a broader dataset could lead to interesting results that may not be spotted with our current configuration.

**Deep learning approaches.** Autoencoders for anomaly detection, LSTMs for sequential transaction modeling, and transformer-based architectures could capture temporal dependencies and complex patterns that tree-based models may miss, particularly for detecting multi-step fraud schemes. Even when literature revealed promising results for these approaches, their implementation was out of our current scope.

**Real-world data validation.** Testing the pipeline on real-world transaction data (if available under appropriate data-sharing agreements) would validate whether the synthetic data findings generalize to production environments with genuine fraud patterns and concept drift.

**Adversarial robustness.** Evaluating model resilience against evolving bot strategies — where attackers adapt their behavioral patterns in response to detection — would assess the durability of the behavioral features as a fraud signal.

**Cost-benefit analysis and cost-sensitive thresholding.** Rather than enforcing a fixed precision constraint, optimizing the expected business cost using false-positive cost ($95 per incident) and false-negative cost ($275 per incident) estimates from the literature would yield operationally optimal thresholds. The precision-constraint sweep (Section 5.3.2) provides the foundation for this analysis; formalizing it as a cost-minimization objective is a natural extension.

**Formal feature ablation.** While the current work uses the three-feature-set comparison as a natural ablation, a more granular study — systematically removing individual features or feature groups and retraining — would quantify the marginal contribution of each feature and identify potential redundancies.

**Model monitoring and drift detection.** Designing a monitoring framework to detect performance degradation over time, including statistical tests on score distributions and automated retraining triggers, would be essential for any production deployment of these models.

Included in the GitHub repository

Included in the GitHub repository

### Appendix C: Per-Seed Results {#appendix-c:-per-seed-results}

Included in the GitHub repository

### Appendix D: Repository Structure and Reproducibility {#appendix-d:-repository-structure-and-reproducibility}

#### File Descriptions {#file-descriptions}

| File                              | Description                                                          |
| :-------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `README.md`                       | Project overview, setup, and reproduction instructions               |
| `requirements.txt`                | Python dependencies with pinned versions                             |
| `behavioral_dataset.py`           | Synthetic behavioral feature generation (4 user profiles)            |
| `fraud_dataset.py`                | Combined synthetic dataset generation (calibrated from PaySim)       |
| `paysim_dataset.py`               | PaySim parameter extraction (distributions, fraud rates)             |
| `training_pipeline.py`            | Reusable ML pipeline: split, encode, train, threshold-tune, evaluate |
| `run_all_models.py`               | Orchestrator: multi-seed, multi-algorithm experiments                |
| `generate_test_graphs.py`         | Test-set performance graphs and literature comparison                |
| `validate_distributions.py`       | Statistical validation: KS tests, QQ plots                           |
| `behavioral_model_training.py`    | Standalone: behavioral-only model                                    |
| `transactional_model_training.py` | Standalone: transactional-only model                                 |
| `combined_model_training.py`      | Standalone: combined model                                           |

#### Reproduction Steps {#reproduction-steps}

1\. Setup

python \-m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate

pip install \-r requirements.txt

2\. Download PaySim dataset from Kaggle and place in data/

3\. Validate synthetic data against PaySim

python validate_distributions.py

4\. Run full experiment suite (5 seeds × 4 algorithms × 3 feature sets)

python run_all_models.py

5\. Generate test set performance graphs and comparison tables

python generate_test_graphs.py

All outputs are saved to the `results/` directory.

Further detailed step by step instructions are included in the `README.md` file.
